  <section id="factor-models">
<span id="id1"></span><h1>Factor Models<a class="headerlink" href="#factor-models" title="Link to this heading">#</a></h1>
<p>This guide covers skfolio’s factor model implementations, their API and their
theoretical foundations. It focuses on the characteristics-based cross-sectional factor
model <a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.prior.CharacteristicsFactorModel.html#skfolio.prior.CharacteristicsFactorModel" title="skfolio.prior.CharacteristicsFactorModel"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">CharacteristicsFactorModel</span></code></a>. This model family has
become foundational in quantitative asset management, and implementing it correctly
requires addressing many practical challenges (e.g. point-in-time data, changing
universes, look-ahead bias, zero-sum constraints, alpha integration, diagnostics,
attribution and computational performance on large universes).</p>
<p>The results in this guide were obtained by fitting a 58-factor US equity model on
the FactSet
<a class="reference external" href="https://www.factset.com/marketplace/catalog/product/factset-fundamentals-point-in-time">Fundamentals Point-in-Time</a>,
<a class="reference external" href="https://www.factset.com/marketplace/catalog/product/factset-estimates-point-in-time-consensus">Estimates Point-in-Time Consensus</a>
and <a class="reference external" href="https://www.factset.com/marketplace/catalog/product/factset-rbics-api">RBICS</a>
datasets. The <a class="reference internal" href="../auto_examples/factor_models/index.html#factor-models-examples"><span class="std std-ref">Factor Models gallery</span></a> provides complete,
runnable tutorials using synthetic characteristics data, so users can explore the full API
while respecting data vendor licences.</p>
<p>For complementary references, see “The Elements of Quantitative Investing” by Giuseppe
Paleologo <a class="footnote-reference brackets" href="#id6" id="id2" role="doc-noteref"><span class="fn-bracket">[</span>1<span class="fn-bracket">]</span></a>, “Active Portfolio Management” by Grinold and Kahn <a class="footnote-reference brackets" href="#id7" id="id3" role="doc-noteref"><span class="fn-bracket">[</span>2<span class="fn-bracket">]</span></a>, and
“Portfolio Optimization: Theory and Application” by Daniel P. Palomar <a class="footnote-reference brackets" href="#id8" id="id4" role="doc-noteref"><span class="fn-bracket">[</span>3<span class="fn-bracket">]</span></a>.</p>
<section id="introduction">
<h2>Introduction<a class="headerlink" href="#introduction" title="Link to this heading">#</a></h2>
<section id="motivation-and-use-cases">
<h3>Motivation and Use Cases<a class="headerlink" href="#motivation-and-use-cases" title="Link to this heading">#</a></h3>
<p>Directly estimating the covariance matrix of a large asset universe is impractical.
The sample covariance matrix of 5,000 assets has over 12 million free parameters and
requires more than 5,000 observations (~20 years of daily data) in order to reach full
rank. The resulting estimate is ill-conditioned, unstable and slow to react to change,
and an optimizer using that estimate will also tend to allocate toward the parts of the
covariance structure where estimation noise is largest.</p>
<p>A factor model addresses this problem by assuming that a small set of pervasive
factors (e.g. market, industries, countries, currencies, styles) drives the
co-movement of assets, and the remainder is asset-specific (i.e. idiosyncratic).
Estimation then reduces to a small factor covariance plus one idiosyncratic variance
per asset, requiring far fewer parameters and a much shorter history.</p>
<p>Factor models are used for:</p>
<ul class="simple">
<li><p><strong>Risk forecasting</strong>: estimate a stable asset covariance matrix for large universes.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Risk decomposition</strong>: express portfolio risk as systematic exposures plus
idiosyncratic risk, separating intended from unintended exposures.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Performance attribution</strong>: explain realized returns factor by factor,
distinguishing systematic factor premia from asset-specific returns, and separating
skill from luck.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Portfolio construction</strong>: supply expected returns, covariance, scenarios and
factor exposures to an optimizer, with exposures that can be monitored and
constrained.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Alpha research</strong>: provide the ingredients of the alpha workflow, such as
idiosyncratic returns as a prediction target, idiosyncratic variances
for signal scaling, and factor exposures for neutralization.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Alpha decomposition</strong>: split an alpha forecast into spanned alpha and orthogonal
alpha before optimization.</p></li>
</ul>
</section>
<section id="model-definition">
<h3>Model Definition<a class="headerlink" href="#model-definition" title="Link to this heading">#</a></h3>
<p>A factor model decomposes asset returns into systematic and idiosyncratic
components:</p>
<div class="math notranslate nohighlight">
\[r_t = B \, f_t + \epsilon_t\]</div>
<p>where:</p>
<ul class="simple">
<li><p><span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(B \in \mathbb{R}^{N \times K}\)</span> is the factor exposure matrix, also called
the loading matrix: the sensitivity of each asset to each factor.</p></li>
<li><p><span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(f_t \in \mathbb{R}^{K}\)</span> is the vector of factor returns: the return per unit
of exposure at time <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(t\)</span>, common to all assets.</p></li>
<li><p><span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(\epsilon_t \in \mathbb{R}^{N}\)</span> is the vector of idiosyncratic returns: the
part of asset returns not explained by the factors.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>The factor structure assumes that common co-movement is captured by the factors, and
that the remaining idiosyncratic covariance is diagonal or sparse. The asset
covariance matrix is:</p>
<div class="math notranslate nohighlight">
\[\Sigma = B \, F \, B^\top + D\]</div>
<p>where <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(F \in \mathbb{R}^{K \times K}\)</span> is the factor covariance matrix and
<span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(D\)</span> is the diagonal or sparse idiosyncratic covariance matrix.</p>
<p>The <a class="reference internal" href="#factor-model-expected-returns"><span class="std std-ref">Expected Returns</span></a> section explains how
<a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.prior.CharacteristicsFactorModel.html#skfolio.prior.CharacteristicsFactorModel" title="skfolio.prior.CharacteristicsFactorModel"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">CharacteristicsFactorModel</span></code></a> estimates expected asset returns
from expected factor returns and an optional alpha forecast.</p>
<p>The rest of this guide covers the estimation of <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(B\)</span>, <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(f_t\)</span>, <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(F\)</span>,
and <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(D\)</span>, and their use in optimization and attribution.</p>
</section>
<section id="types-of-factor-models">
<h3>Types of Factor Models<a class="headerlink" href="#types-of-factor-models" title="Link to this heading">#</a></h3>
<p>Factor model families differ by what is observed before fitting: factor returns,
factor exposures, or neither.</p>
<p><strong>Time-series factor models</strong> observe the factor returns and estimate asset exposures.
Factors are observable time series such as factor ETF returns, long-short factor portfolio
returns, or macroeconomic series (e.g. inflation, rates, GDP growth), hence the
alternative name “macroeconomic factor models”. Each asset’s exposures come from a
time-series regression of its returns on those factor series. They are interpretable,
straightforward to estimate, and they can work on small universes because each
asset is estimated independently. However, they require long return histories per asset,
exposures react slowly to asset-level change because they only update through the
regression window, and the risk of misspecification is higher. When factors are
tradable, their return history can be used to estimate factor premia. For
non-tradable variables, factor premia are estimated with a second-pass
cross-sectional procedure such as Fama-MacBeth on a sufficiently broad universe.
Implemented in <a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.prior.TimeSeriesFactorModel.html#skfolio.prior.TimeSeriesFactorModel" title="skfolio.prior.TimeSeriesFactorModel"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">TimeSeriesFactorModel</span></code></a>.</p>
<p><strong>Characteristics-based factor models</strong>, also called “fundamental factor models”, observe
exposures and estimate factor returns. Exposures are built from point-in-time
asset characteristics (e.g. industry classification, country, market capitalization,
book equity, sales, operating cash flow, analyst estimates), and factor returns are derived
from one cross-sectional regression of asset returns on exposures at each date.
Exposures react immediately to asset-level change, new assets need no return history to
receive exposures, and factors can be neutralized against each other to produce pure
factor definitions. The challenges of this model are the complexity of the estimation
procedure and the heavier data requirement, both of which are covered in this guide.
Implemented in <a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.prior.CharacteristicsFactorModel.html#skfolio.prior.CharacteristicsFactorModel" title="skfolio.prior.CharacteristicsFactorModel"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">CharacteristicsFactorModel</span></code></a>.</p>
<div class="admonition note">
<p class="admonition-title">Note</p>
<p>The Fama-French procedure also starts from characteristics, but follows a different
construction. It uses characteristics to sort assets into quantile long-short
portfolios whose returns define the factors. It is primarily a factor-pricing
framework for explaining the cross-section of expected returns, as opposed to a full
risk model. To use those factor returns as a risk model for arbitrary assets, exposures
must be estimated by time-series regression, inheriting the drawbacks of
time-series factor models.</p>
<p>Moreover, quantile portfolios are not pure factors in the characteristics
risk-model sense. A value portfolio, for example, can also carry industry, size,
profitability, investment or momentum tilts because the sorting procedure only
controls the characteristics used to build the portfolio. Intersection procedures
reduce this contamination by sorting on several characteristics at the same time,
but they do not scale to a large number of factors.</p>
</div>
<p><strong>Statistical factor models</strong> observe neither factor returns nor exposures and instead
extract both from asset returns using methods such as PCA. They are adaptive and need
returns only, but factors have no economic identity and may drift across regimes.
Currently not implemented in skfolio.</p>
</section>
<section id="historical-background">
<h3>Historical Background<a class="headerlink" href="#historical-background" title="Link to this heading">#</a></h3>
<p>Characteristics factor models originate with Barr Rosenberg, whose 1974 work on
extra-market components of covariance <a class="footnote-reference brackets" href="#id9" id="id5" role="doc-noteref"><span class="fn-bracket">[</span>4<span class="fn-bracket">]</span></a> showed that asset characteristics predict
return covariation, and that a small set of common factors explains most
cross-sectional variation while the residual is asset-specific. Rosenberg founded
Barra (now part of MSCI) to commercialize the approach, and the first US equity model
(USE1) was released in 1975. Specialist competitors followed, including Northfield
(1985), Axioma (1998) and Wolfe Research (2008). Data vendors later entered the market
by adding proprietary factor models to their data and analytics platforms, including
Bloomberg, FactSet, S&amp;P Global and Morningstar. Factor risk models have since become
standard infrastructure across quantitative hedge funds, asset managers and banks.</p>
<p>These models were initially built for risk estimation, but practitioners observed
that some risk factors (e.g. value, momentum) also earn persistent premia. Modern
implementations therefore estimate expected factor returns alongside factor
covariances, enabling deliberate tilts toward specific factors in optimization, the
foundation of what became known as “smart beta”.</p>
<p>Today, commercial factor risk models are often distributed as model catalogues, usually segmented by
region and horizon. skfolio now provides a toolkit for building and customizing factor models directly
from user-defined data, rather than selecting a single predefined model from a catalogue. This enables a
continuum of specifications, recognizing the view that there is no “one size fits all” factor model.</p>
</section>
<section id="alternatives-to-a-single-centralized-factor-model">
<h3>Alternatives to a Single Centralized Factor Model<a class="headerlink" href="#alternatives-to-a-single-centralized-factor-model" title="Link to this heading">#</a></h3>
<p>A factor model is an estimate. Its exposures, factor covariance and
idiosyncratic variances carry estimation error, and its specification is never
complete, as no factor set captures all common co-movement.</p>
<p>The traditional commercial offering can lead to a single house risk model
being shared by all strategies and risk management teams. skfolio supports
this approach but also allows for an alternative approach where the factor model is
itself an estimator parameter, such that each strategy can embed its own specific
factor model. The strategy and its factor model then form a single meta-model that is fitted,
evaluated and tuned jointly.</p>
<p>Multiple factor models allow portfolio construction and risk monitoring to use different model
specifications. A portfolio may be constructed with a factor model tailored to its universe,
horizon and alpha process, while risk management evaluates exposures and covariance with a
broader firm-wide model. At the book level, using different factor models across portfolios
and strategies introduces model diversification. Estimation error and specification error are
then distributed across model specifications rather than concentrated in a single shared model.</p>
</section>
</section>
<section id="model-overview">
<h2>Model Overview<a class="headerlink" href="#model-overview" title="Link to this heading">#</a></h2>
<section id="estimation-pipeline">
<h3>Estimation Pipeline<a class="headerlink" href="#estimation-pipeline" title="Link to this heading">#</a></h3>
<p><a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.prior.CharacteristicsFactorModel.html#skfolio.prior.CharacteristicsFactorModel" title="skfolio.prior.CharacteristicsFactorModel"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">CharacteristicsFactorModel</span></code></a> is a meta-estimator following
scikit-learn conventions. It composes sub-estimators, descriptors, factor exposure
estimators, a cross-sectional regressor, prior estimators for factor and idiosyncratic
risk and an optional alpha estimator, each of which can be replaced, tuned and
customized independently. The input is a point-in-time
<a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.containers.AssetPanel.html#skfolio.containers.AssetPanel" title="skfolio.containers.AssetPanel"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">AssetPanel</span></code></a> of asset characteristics and the output is a
<a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.prior.ReturnDistribution.html#skfolio.prior.ReturnDistribution" title="skfolio.prior.ReturnDistribution"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">ReturnDistribution</span></code></a> consumed by all skfolio optimizations,
together with a <a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.prior.FactorModel.html#skfolio.prior.FactorModel" title="skfolio.prior.FactorModel"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">FactorModel</span></code></a> container holding the full
decomposition and diagnostics.</p>
<p>The model is fitted as follows:</p>
<ol class="arabic simple">
<li><p>Start from point-in-time asset characteristics stored as panel fields (e.g.
<code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">returns</span></code>, <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">market_cap</span></code>, <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">book_equity</span></code>, <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">industry</span></code>).</p></li>
<li><p>Compute descriptor values from these fields, or pass through existing fields
unchanged, using descriptor estimators.</p></li>
<li><p>Build factor exposures. Style factors are typically formed by combining one or
more descriptors and applying cross-sectional transformations (e.g. winsorization,
z-scoring). Categorical factors (e.g. industry, country, currency) are represented
by one-hot exposures.</p></li>
<li><p>Orthogonalize selected exposures against other factors or families when
<code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">neutralize_against</span></code> is provided.</p></li>
<li><p>Reparameterize constrained families when <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">constrained_families</span></code> is provided. This
enforces the benchmark-weighted zero-sum constraint on factor returns within each
constrained family and produces a full-rank basis for factor-level estimators.</p></li>
<li><p>Estimate realized factor returns with
<code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">cs_regressor</span></code> on the estimation universe defined by the panel’s
<code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">estimation_mask</span></code>. By default, regression weights are based on market
capitalization through <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">regression_mcap_power</span></code>. When
<code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">inv_idio_variance_weight_shrinkage</span> <span class="pre">&gt;</span> <span class="pre">0</span></code>, a two-pass procedure blends those weights
with inverse-idiosyncratic-variance weights estimated from first-pass residuals.</p></li>
<li><p>Estimate the factor return distribution with <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">factor_prior_estimator</span></code>, including
expected factor returns (factor premia), factor covariance and factor return
scenarios. This step can introduce factor covariance shrinkage, short-term
volatility updating or Newey-West HAC correction.</p></li>
<li><p>Estimate idiosyncratic variances with <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">idio_variance_estimator</span></code>, then form the
idiosyncratic covariance as a diagonal matrix or, when <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">idio_corr_threshold</span> <span class="pre">&gt;</span> <span class="pre">0</span></code>,
as a sparse covariance using correlation thresholding.</p></li>
<li><p>If provided, fit <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">alpha_estimator</span></code> to produce an alpha forecast. Decompose it into
spanned alpha and orthogonal alpha, blend factor-implied asset expected returns with
the spanned alpha using <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">spanned_alpha_shrinkage</span></code>, shrink the orthogonal alpha
with <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">orthogonal_alpha_confidence</span></code> and assemble the final <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(\mu\)</span>,
<span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(\Sigma\)</span> and asset return scenarios on the investment universe.</p></li>
</ol>
<p>Each step is detailed in the following sections.</p>
</section>
<section id="code-example">
<span id="factor-model-code-example"></span><h3>Code Example<a class="headerlink" href="#code-example" title="Link to this heading">#</a></h3>
<p>The model below is used throughout this guide. It is a medium-horizon US equity model
with 58 factors: 1 global factor, 44 industry factors and 13 style factors built from
29 descriptors. It uses within-industry scoring, neutralization, a zero-sum constraint
on the industry family, regression weights that blend market-capitalization weights
with inverse-idiosyncratic-variance weights and a regime-adjusted factor covariance
estimator. The model was fitted on the FactSet point-in-time datasets of 2,000 US
equities with daily data from 2013 to 2026. The parametrization is intentionally
simple and has not been fine-tuned. This simplified model is used as the standard
example for presenting the API. See <a class="reference internal" href="#factor-model-hyper-parameter-tuning"><span class="std std-ref">Hyperparameter Tuning</span></a> for guidance on tuning the model to your data
and goals.</p>
<div class="highlight-python notranslate"><div class="highlight"><pre><span></span><span class="kn">from</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="nn">skfolio.descriptor</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="kn">import</span> <span class="p">(</span>

<span class="kn">from</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="nn">skfolio.factor_exposure</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="kn">import</span> <span class="p">(</span>
    <span class="n">DerivedFactor</span><span class="p">,</span>

    <span class="n">GlobalFactor</span><span class="p">,</span>
    <span class="n">OneHotCategoricalFactors</span><span class="p">,</span>
<span class="p">)</span>
<span class="kn">from</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="nn">skfolio.moments</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="kn">import</span> <span class="n">EWMu</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="n">RegimeAdjustedEWCovariance</span>
<span class="kn">from</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="nn">skfolio.prior</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="kn">import</span> <span class="n">CharacteristicsFactorModel</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="n">EmpiricalPrior</span>

<span class="n">month</span> <span class="o">=</span> <span class="mi">21</span>
<span class="n">quarter</span> <span class="o">=</span> <span class="mi">3</span> <span class="o">*</span> <span class="n">month</span>

    <span class="n">descriptors</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="p">[(</span><span class="s2">"market_beta"</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="n">EWMarketBeta</span><span class="p">(</span><span class="n">half_life</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="n">year</span><span class="p">))],</span>

    <span class="n">descriptors</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="p">[(</span><span class="s2">"momentum"</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="n">EWMomentum</span><span class="p">(</span><span class="n">half_life</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="n">half_year</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="n">skip</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="n">month</span><span class="p">))],</span>

<span class="p">)</span>

<span class="n">non_linear_size_factor</span> <span class="o">=</span> <span class="n">DerivedFactor</span><span class="p">(</span>
    <span class="n">source</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="s2">"size"</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="n">func</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="k">lambda</span> <span class="n">x</span><span class="p">:</span> <span class="n">x</span><span class="o">**</span><span class="mi">3</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="n">transform_by_group</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="s2">"industry"</span>
<span class="p">)</span>

<span class="n">value_factor</span> <span class="o">=</span> <span class="n">FixedWeightedFactor</span><span class="p">(</span>
    <span class="n">descriptors</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="p">[</span>
        <span class="p">(</span><span class="s2">"book_to_price"</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="n">BookToPrice</span><span class="p">()),</span>
        <span class="p">(</span><span class="s2">"sales_to_price"</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="n">SalesToPrice</span><span class="p">()),</span>
        <span class="p">(</span><span class="s2">"cash_flow_to_price"</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="n">CashFlowToPrice</span><span class="p">()),</span>
    <span class="p">],</span>
    <span class="n">weights</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="p">[</span><span class="mf">0.8</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="mf">0.1</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="mf">0.1</span><span class="p">],</span>
    <span class="n">transform_by_group</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="s2">"industry"</span><span class="p">,</span>
<span class="p">)</span>

<span class="n">earnings_yield_factor</span> <span class="o">=</span> <span class="n">FixedWeightedFactor</span><span class="p">(</span>
    <span class="n">descriptors</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="p">[</span>
        <span class="p">(</span><span class="s2">"fwd_earnings_to_price"</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="n">ForwardEarningsToPrice</span><span class="p">()),</span>
        <span class="p">(</span><span class="s2">"earnings_to_price"</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="n">EarningsToPrice</span><span class="p">()),</span>
        <span class="p">(</span><span class="s2">"enterprise_multiple"</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="n">EbitdaToEnterpriseValue</span><span class="p">()),</span>
    <span class="p">],</span>
    <span class="n">transform_by_group</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="s2">"industry"</span><span class="p">,</span>
<span class="p">)</span>

<span class="n">growth_factor</span> <span class="o">=</span> <span class="n">FixedWeightedFactor</span><span class="p">(</span>
    <span class="n">descriptors</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="p">[</span>
        <span class="p">(</span><span class="s2">"earnings_change_to_price"</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="n">EarningsChangeToPrice</span><span class="p">(</span><span class="n">lag</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="n">year</span><span class="p">)),</span>
        <span class="p">(</span><span class="s2">"sales_growth"</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="n">SalesGrowthRate</span><span class="p">(</span><span class="n">lag</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="n">year</span><span class="p">)),</span>
    <span class="p">],</span>
    <span class="n">transform_by_group</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="s2">"industry"</span><span class="p">,</span>
<span class="p">)</span>

<span class="n">profitability_factor</span> <span class="o">=</span> <span class="n">FixedWeightedFactor</span><span class="p">(</span>
    <span class="n">descriptors</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="p">[</span>
        <span class="p">(</span><span class="s2">"asset_turnover"</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="n">AssetTurnover</span><span class="p">()),</span>
        <span class="p">(</span><span class="s2">"gross_profitability"</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="n">GrossProfitability</span><span class="p">()),</span>
        <span class="p">(</span><span class="s2">"gross_margin"</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="n">GrossMargin</span><span class="p">()),</span>
        <span class="p">(</span><span class="s2">"return_on_assets"</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="n">ReturnOnAssets</span><span class="p">()),</span>
        <span class="p">(</span><span class="s2">"return_on_equity"</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="n">ReturnOnEquity</span><span class="p">()),</span>
        <span class="p">(</span><span class="s2">"cash_flow_to_assets"</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="n">CashFlowToAssets</span><span class="p">()),</span>
    <span class="p">],</span>
    <span class="n">transform_by_group</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="s2">"industry"</span><span class="p">,</span>

<span class="n">investment_factor</span> <span class="o">=</span> <span class="n">FixedWeightedFactor</span><span class="p">(</span>
    <span class="n">descriptors</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="p">[</span>
        <span class="p">(</span><span class="s2">"asset_growth"</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="n">AssetsGrowthRate</span><span class="p">(</span><span class="n">lag</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="n">year</span><span class="p">)),</span>

    <span class="n">transform_by_group</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="s2">"industry"</span><span class="p">,</span>

    <span class="n">weights</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="p">[</span><span class="mf">0.7</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="mf">0.3</span><span class="p">],</span>

        <span class="p">(</span><span class="s2">"book_leverage"</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="n">BookLeverage</span><span class="p">()),</span>
    <span class="p">],</span>
    <span class="n">transform_by_group</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="s2">"industry"</span><span class="p">,</span>
<span class="p">)</span>

<span class="n">liquidity_factor</span> <span class="o">=</span> <span class="n">FixedWeightedFactor</span><span class="p">(</span>
    <span class="n">descriptors</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="p">[</span>
        <span class="p">(</span><span class="s2">"share_turnover"</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="n">EWShareTurnover</span><span class="p">(</span><span class="n">half_life</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="n">quarter</span><span class="p">)),</span>
        <span class="p">(</span><span class="s2">"amihud_illiquidity"</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="n">EWAmihudIlliquidity</span><span class="p">()),</span>
    <span class="p">],</span>
    <span class="n">transform_by_group</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="s2">"industry"</span><span class="p">,</span>
<span class="p">)</span>

<span class="n">volatility_factor</span> <span class="o">=</span> <span class="n">FixedWeightedFactor</span><span class="p">(</span>
    <span class="n">descriptors</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="p">[</span>
        <span class="p">(</span><span class="s2">"vol"</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="n">EWVolatility</span><span class="p">(</span><span class="n">half_life</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="n">quarter</span><span class="p">)),</span>
        <span class="p">(</span>
            <span class="s2">"residual_vol"</span><span class="p">,</span>
            <span class="n">EWResidualVolatility</span><span class="p">(</span><span class="n">half_life</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="n">quarter</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="n">beta_half_life</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="n">quarter</span><span class="p">),</span>
        <span class="p">),</span>
    <span class="p">],</span>
    <span class="n">transform_by_group</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="s2">"industry"</span><span class="p">,</span>
<span class="p">)</span>

<span class="c1"># Characteristics Factor Model</span>
<span class="n">model</span> <span class="o">=</span> <span class="n">CharacteristicsFactorModel</span><span class="p">(</span>
    <span class="n">factors</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="p">[</span>
        <span class="p">(</span><span class="s2">"market"</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="n">global_factor</span><span class="p">),</span>
        <span class="p">(</span><span class="s2">"industry"</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="n">industry_factors</span><span class="p">),</span>
        <span class="p">(</span><span class="s2">"beta"</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="n">beta_factor</span><span class="p">),</span>
        <span class="p">(</span><span class="s2">"momentum"</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="n">momentum_factor</span><span class="p">),</span>
        <span class="p">(</span><span class="s2">"size"</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="n">size_factor</span><span class="p">),</span>
        <span class="p">(</span><span class="s2">"non_linear_size"</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="n">non_linear_size_factor</span><span class="p">),</span>
        <span class="p">(</span><span class="s2">"value"</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="n">value_factor</span><span class="p">),</span>
        <span class="p">(</span><span class="s2">"earnings_yield"</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="n">earnings_yield_factor</span><span class="p">),</span>
        <span class="p">(</span><span class="s2">"growth"</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="n">growth_factor</span><span class="p">),</span>
        <span class="p">(</span><span class="s2">"profitability"</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="n">profitability_factor</span><span class="p">),</span>
        <span class="p">(</span><span class="s2">"investment"</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="n">investment_factor</span><span class="p">),</span>
        <span class="p">(</span><span class="s2">"dividend_yield"</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="n">dividend_yield_factor</span><span class="p">),</span>
        <span class="p">(</span><span class="s2">"leverage"</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="n">leverage_factor</span><span class="p">),</span>
        <span class="p">(</span><span class="s2">"liquidity"</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="n">liquidity_factor</span><span class="p">),</span>
        <span class="p">(</span><span class="s2">"volatility"</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="n">volatility_factor</span><span class="p">),</span>
    <span class="p">],</span>
    <span class="n">neutralize_against</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="p">{</span>
        <span class="s2">"non_linear_size"</span><span class="p">:</span> <span class="p">[</span><span class="s2">"size"</span><span class="p">],</span>
        <span class="s2">"volatility"</span><span class="p">:</span> <span class="p">[</span><span class="s2">"beta"</span><span class="p">],</span>
    <span class="p">},</span>
    <span class="n">constrained_families</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="p">[(</span><span class="s2">"industry"</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="kc">None</span><span class="p">)],</span>
    <span class="n">exposure_lag</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="mi">1</span><span class="p">,</span>
    <span class="n">inv_idio_variance_weight_shrinkage</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="mf">0.5</span><span class="p">,</span>
    <span class="n">factor_prior_estimator</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="n">EmpiricalPrior</span><span class="p">(</span>
        <span class="n">covariance_estimator</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="n">RegimeAdjustedEWCovariance</span><span class="p">(</span>
            <span class="n">half_life</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="n">half_year</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="n">corr_half_life</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="n">year</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="n">regime_half_life</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="n">month</span>
        <span class="p">),</span>
        <span class="n">mu_estimator</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="n">EWMu</span><span class="p">(</span><span class="n">half_life</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="n">year</span><span class="p">),</span>
    <span class="p">),</span>
    <span class="n">n_jobs</span><span class="o">=-</span><span class="mi">1</span><span class="p">,</span>
<span class="p">)</span>
</pre></div>
</div>
<p>The model is fitted on a point-in-time <a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.containers.AssetPanel.html#skfolio.containers.AssetPanel" title="skfolio.containers.AssetPanel"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">AssetPanel</span></code></a> whose
construction is covered in the <a class="reference internal" href="#factor-model-input-data"><span class="std std-ref">Input Data</span></a> section:</p>
<div class="highlight-python notranslate"><div class="highlight"><pre><span></span><span class="n">model</span><span class="o">.</span><span class="n">fit</span><span class="p">(</span><span class="n">characteristics</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="n">characteristics</span><span class="p">)</span>
</pre></div>
</div>
<p>For incremental updates on new observations, use <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">partial_fit</span></code>, covered in the
<a class="reference internal" href="#factor-model-online-learning"><span class="std std-ref">Online Learning</span></a> section:</p>
<div class="highlight-python notranslate"><div class="highlight"><pre><span></span><span class="n">model</span><span class="o">.</span><span class="n">partial_fit</span><span class="p">(</span><span class="n">characteristics</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="n">new_characteristics</span><span class="p">)</span>
</pre></div>
</div>
<p>After fitting, the model exposes two main attributes.</p>
<p><code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">return_distribution_</span></code> is the <a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.prior.ReturnDistribution.html#skfolio.prior.ReturnDistribution" title="skfolio.prior.ReturnDistribution"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">ReturnDistribution</span></code></a> consumed by
skfolio optimizations. It contains the expected asset returns <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">mu</span></code>, the asset
covariance matrix <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">covariance</span></code> and the asset return scenarios <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">returns</span></code>, all on the
investment universe. Assets that are not investable at the current point in time
(e.g. delisted, not yet listed, or still in <a class="reference internal" href="#factor-model-warmup"><span class="std std-ref">warmup</span></a>)
are represented with NaN.</p>
<p><code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">factor_model_</span></code> is the <a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.prior.FactorModel.html#skfolio.prior.FactorModel" title="skfolio.prior.FactorModel"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">FactorModel</span></code></a> container holding the full
decomposition: factor exposures, loading matrix, factor returns, expected factor
returns and factor covariance, idiosyncratic returns, variances and covariance,
regression and benchmark weights. It provides DataFrame accessors (<code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">factor_returns_df</span></code>,
<code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">idio_returns_df</span></code>, <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">exposures_df</span></code>), slicing (<code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">select_assets</span></code>, <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">select_observations</span></code>),
a <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">summary</span></code> method and the diagnostic statistics and plots covered in the
<a class="reference internal" href="#factor-model-diagnostics"><span class="std std-ref">Diagnostics</span></a> section.</p>
<div class="highlight-python notranslate"><div class="highlight"><pre><span></span><span class="n">distribution</span> <span class="o">=</span> <span class="n">model</span><span class="o">.</span><span class="n">return_distribution_</span>
<span class="n">distribution</span><span class="o">.</span><span class="n">mu</span>          <span class="c1"># expected returns, shape (n_assets,)</span>
<span class="n">distribution</span><span class="o">.</span><span class="n">covariance</span>  <span class="c1"># asset covariance, shape (n_assets, n_assets)</span>
<span class="n">distribution</span><span class="o">.</span><span class="n">returns</span>     <span class="c1"># return scenarios, shape (n_observations, n_assets)</span>

<span class="n">factor_model</span> <span class="o">=</span> <span class="n">model</span><span class="o">.</span><span class="n">factor_model_</span>
<span class="n">factor_model</span><span class="o">.</span><span class="n">summary</span><span class="p">()</span>
<span class="n">factor_model</span><span class="o">.</span><span class="n">factor_returns_df</span><span class="p">()</span>
</pre></div>
</div>
<p>The fitted sub-estimators are also available with the usual scikit-learn trailing
underscore convention: <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">cs_regressor_</span></code>, <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">factor_prior_estimator_</span></code>,
<code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">idio_variance_estimator_</span></code>, <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">idio_corr_estimator_</span></code> and <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">alpha_estimator_</span></code>.</p>
<p>The <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">X</span></code> argument of <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">fit</span></code> is optional. When provided, <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">X</span></code> selects the investment
universe: its columns define the assets returned in <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">return_distribution_</span></code> and
<code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">factor_model_</span></code>. Factor estimation still uses the <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">returns</span></code> field of
<code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">characteristics</span></code>, which can cover a broader point-in-time universe. This keeps the
estimator compatible with skfolio pipelines, cross-validation, prediction and scoring.
When <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">X</span></code> is <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">None</span></code>, the investment universe equals the coverage universe.</p>
</section>
</section>
<section id="input-data">
<span id="factor-model-input-data"></span><h2>Input Data<a class="headerlink" href="#input-data" title="Link to this heading">#</a></h2>
<p>The model consumes a point-in-time <a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.containers.AssetPanel.html#skfolio.containers.AssetPanel" title="skfolio.containers.AssetPanel"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">AssetPanel</span></code></a> of asset
characteristics. The panel must include a <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">returns</span></code> field and, when market-cap
weighting is used, a <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">market_cap</span></code> field. The remaining fields depend on the chosen
descriptors and alpha estimators. The <a class="reference internal" href="#factor-model-code-example"><span class="std std-ref">example</span></a>
model uses market data (e.g.
<code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">adj_close</span></code>, <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">adj_volume</span></code>, <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">adj_shares_outstanding</span></code>), fundamentals (e.g.
<code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">book_equity</span></code>, <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">sales_ttm</span></code>, <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">total_assets</span></code>, <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">operating_cash_flow_ttm</span></code>), analyst
estimates (e.g. <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">eps_ntm</span></code>, <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">dps_ntm</span></code>) and a categorical <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">industry</span></code> field.</p>
<section id="assetpanel-container">
<h3>AssetPanel Container<a class="headerlink" href="#assetpanel-container" title="Link to this heading">#</a></h3>
<p>A factor model pipeline applies many cross-sectional and time-series transformations
to the same date-by-asset data. With general-purpose containers (e.g. DataFrames, xarray),
each step must re-align indexes, group by date or pivot before computing. This adds
overhead and increases the risk of indexing errors, either on the time index, which
can introduce look-ahead bias, or on the asset index. To address this, skfolio provides
<a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.containers.AssetPanel.html#skfolio.containers.AssetPanel" title="skfolio.containers.AssetPanel"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">AssetPanel</span></code></a>, a dedicated container for aligned
cross-sectional asset data. An <a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.containers.AssetPanel.html#skfolio.containers.AssetPanel" title="skfolio.containers.AssetPanel"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">AssetPanel</span></code></a> validates
alignment once so that estimators operate on already-aligned numeric arrays and stores
universe masks explicitly, which is needed for point-in-time estimation on changing
universes (e.g. listing, delisting, inclusion/exclusion rules).</p>
<p>The rationale behind the container, the wide-format convention and the NaN-handling
conventions are covered in <a class="reference internal" href="data_representation.html#asset-data-representation"><span class="std std-ref">Asset Data Representation</span></a>.</p>
<p>Every field shares the same two axes with shape <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">(n_observations,</span> <span class="pre">n_assets)</span></code>. Three
kinds of fields are supported:</p>
<ul class="simple">
<li><p>2D numeric fields (e.g. <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">returns</span></code>, <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">market_cap</span></code>)</p></li>
<li><p>2D categorical fields (e.g. <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">industry</span></code>, <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">country</span></code>), stored as integer codes with
their category labels</p></li>
<li><p>3D numeric fields (e.g. factor exposures), with labeled third axis</p></li>
</ul>
<p>The panel also carries two boolean masks aligned with the data, <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">active_mask</span></code> and
<code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">estimation_mask</span></code>, described in <a class="reference internal" href="#factor-model-universes"><span class="std std-ref">Coverage, Estimation and Investment
Universes</span></a>.</p>
<p>This layout has additional practical benefits:</p>
<ul class="simple">
<li><p>The container is scikit-learn compatible: <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">len(panel)</span></code> returns the number of
observations and <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">panel[start:stop]</span></code> returns a zero-copy view, so the panel can be
passed directly to cross-validation and hyper-parameter tuning utilities, and
walk-forward folds reuse the same field arrays instead of copying them.</p></li>
<li><p>With thread-based parallelism, workers read the same panel in memory instead of
receiving separate process copies, which is significant for large panels.</p></li>
<li><p>Panels are saved as <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">.npy</span></code> files and support memory-mapped loading for fast
startup on large datasets.</p></li>
</ul>
<div class="highlight-python notranslate"><div class="highlight"><pre><span></span><span class="kn">import</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="nn">numpy</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="k">as</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="nn">np</span>
<span class="kn">from</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="nn">skfolio.containers</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="kn">import</span> <span class="n">AssetPanel</span>
<span class="kn">from</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="nn">skfolio.datasets</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="kn">import</span> <span class="n">make_synthetic_characteristics</span>

<span class="c1"># Build a panel from aligned date-by-asset arrays.</span>
<span class="n">panel</span> <span class="o">=</span> <span class="n">AssetPanel</span><span class="p">(</span>
    <span class="n">fields</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="p">{</span>
        <span class="s2">"returns"</span><span class="p">:</span> <span class="n">returns</span><span class="p">,</span>        <span class="c1"># ndarray (n_observations, n_assets)</span>
        <span class="s2">"market_cap"</span><span class="p">:</span> <span class="n">market_cap</span><span class="p">,</span>  <span class="c1"># ndarray (n_observations, n_assets)</span>
    <span class="p">},</span>
    <span class="n">observations</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="n">dates</span><span class="p">,</span>
    <span class="n">asset_names</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="n">assets</span><span class="p">,</span>
    <span class="n">active_mask</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="n">active_mask</span><span class="p">,</span>
    <span class="n">estimation_mask</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="n">estimation_mask</span><span class="p">,</span>
<span class="p">)</span>
<span class="n">panel</span><span class="o">.</span><span class="n">add_categorical_field</span><span class="p">(</span>
    <span class="n">name</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="s2">"industry"</span><span class="p">,</span>
    <span class="n">values</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="n">industry_codes</span><span class="p">,</span>  <span class="c1"># integer codes (n_observations, n_assets)</span>
    <span class="n">levels</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="p">[</span><span class="s2">"energy"</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="s2">"bank"</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="s2">"technology"</span><span class="p">],</span>
<span class="p">)</span>

<span class="c1"># Inspect dimensions, fields, missing values and mask coverage.</span>
<span class="n">panel</span><span class="o">.</span><span class="n">info</span><span class="p">()</span>

<span class="c1"># Save and reload a panel.</span>
<span class="n">panel</span><span class="o">.</span><span class="n">save</span><span class="p">(</span><span class="s2">"path/to/saved_panel"</span><span class="p">)</span>
<span class="n">saved_panel</span> <span class="o">=</span> <span class="n">AssetPanel</span><span class="o">.</span><span class="n">load</span><span class="p">(</span><span class="s2">"path/to/saved_panel"</span><span class="p">)</span>

<span class="c1"># Generate a synthetic panel for examples and tests.</span>
<span class="n">characteristics</span> <span class="o">=</span> <span class="n">make_synthetic_characteristics</span><span class="p">()</span>
</pre></div>
</div>
</section>
<section id="coverage-estimation-and-investment-universes">
<span id="factor-model-universes"></span><h3>Coverage, Estimation and Investment Universes<a class="headerlink" href="#coverage-estimation-and-investment-universes" title="Link to this heading">#</a></h3>
<p>A factor model distinguishes three universes:</p>
<ul class="simple">
<li><p>The <strong>coverage universe</strong> is the full set of assets stored in the
<a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.containers.AssetPanel.html#skfolio.containers.AssetPanel" title="skfolio.containers.AssetPanel"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">AssetPanel</span></code></a>. It contains the estimation universe and
the investment universe.</p></li>
<li><p>The <strong>estimation universe</strong>, defined by the panel’s <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">estimation_mask</span></code>, is the
subset of observation-asset pairs used to fit cross-sectional statistics,
factor-return regressions, benchmark and regression weights, alpha estimators and
regime statistics. Pairs outside it still receive transformed values, exposures and
forecasts, but do not contribute to those fitted statistics.</p></li>
<li><p>The <strong>investment universe</strong> is the set of assets selected for portfolio
optimization. It is returned in <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">return_distribution_</span></code> and
<code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">factor_model_</span></code>. When <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">X</span></code> is provided to <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">fit</span></code>, its columns select this universe.
When <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">X</span></code> is <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">None</span></code>, the investment universe equals the coverage universe. This lets
the model estimate factors on a broader cross-section, then return outputs only for
the assets used downstream.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>Membership within the coverage universe varies through time and is tracked by the
panel’s <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">active_mask</span></code>. <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">False</span></code> marks an asset outside the universe at that date
(e.g. pre-listing, post-delisting), while <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">True</span></code> with a NaN value marks missing data
for an active asset (e.g. holiday, missing quote). The <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">estimation_mask</span></code> is enforced
as a subset of <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">active_mask</span></code>.</p>
<p>The estimation universe must be broad enough to represent the investment opportunity
set, liquid enough to avoid spurious return relationships and stable enough for factor
exposures to behave consistently through time. For a US equity model, a typical
estimation universe is 1,000 to 3,000 names. It should also be large enough relative to the
factor set. The number of estimation assets <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(N\)</span> should be well above the number of
factors <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(K\)</span> for a stable cross-sectional regression, and best practice is to have
at least 20 estimation assets per industry (e.g. 50 industries require at least
1,000 assets in a well-balanced universe, and more if some industries are sparsely
represented).</p>
<p>The effective size of the estimation universe also depends on the regression weights.
The default square-root market-capitalization weights are commonly used as a proxy for
inverse idiosyncratic variance. Under this default weighting, the contribution of
small stocks decreases rapidly: relative to the full universe of investable US
equities, the largest 2,000 stocks represent about 99% of total market capitalization
and would carry roughly 90% of the total regression weight. Extending the universe
beyond this point can still be useful when the additional names improve estimation
of small-cap-sensitive factors or increase coverage of sparsely represented industries.
Otherwise, extending the estimation universe further mostly adds memory and compute cost
and may add noise from illiquid securities (e.g. stale prices, zero-return days,
bid-ask bounce, missing fundamentals).</p>
</section>
<section id="point-in-time-data">
<span id="factor-model-point-in-time-data"></span><h3>Point-in-Time Data<a class="headerlink" href="#point-in-time-data" title="Link to this heading">#</a></h3>
<p>AssetPanel characteristics should be built from point-in-time (PIT) datasets. For
fundamentals, this means respecting reporting lags and using the figures as reported
at the time, before later restatements. Data vendors offer point-in-time datasets for
this purpose, and the example in this guide is constructed using FactSet point-in-time datasets.
When actual availability dates are not available, a common fallback is to apply a
conservative lag from the fiscal period end, such as 90 days, before making the value
available to the model.</p>
<p>Let’s suppose ABC’s fiscal Q2 ends on 2024-06-30, Q2 sales are reported as 100m
on 2024-08-05, and later restated to 96m on 2024-11-12. If the latest available
Q1 sales value is 90m before the Q2 report, a daily <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">AssetPanel</span></code> stores:</p>
<div class="pst-scrollable-table-container"><table class="table">
<thead>
<tr class="row-odd"><th class="head"><p>Observation date</p></th>
<th class="head"><p>Sales value in the PIT panel</p></th>
<th class="head"><p>Source available on that date</p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr class="row-even"><td><p>2024-08-01</p></td>
<td><p>90m</p></td>
<td><p>Latest available Q1 value</p></td>
</tr>
<tr class="row-odd"><td><p>2024-08-02</p></td>
<td><p>90m</p></td>
<td><p>Latest available Q1 value</p></td>
</tr>
<tr class="row-even"><td><p>2024-08-05</p></td>
<td><p>100m</p></td>
<td><p>2024 Q2 report</p></td>
</tr>
<tr class="row-odd"><td><p>2024-08-06</p></td>
<td><p>100m</p></td>
<td><p>Latest available Q2 value</p></td>
</tr>
<tr class="row-even"><td><p>2024-11-12</p></td>
<td><p>96m</p></td>
<td><p>2024 Q2 restatement</p></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
</div>
<p>Universe membership must also be point-in-time. The universe is formed by applying
eligibility rules at each observation date, using fields known on that date such as
listing status, market capitalization, liquidity, sector, country, exchange and
security type. Fitting history on the current constituent list introduces survivorship
bias as assets that were delisted, acquired or defaulted disappear from the sample,
biasing both estimates. See
<a class="reference internal" href="data_representation.html#asset-data-representation"><span class="std std-ref">data representation</span></a> for missing data, changing
universes and <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">active_mask</span></code>.</p>
</section>
<section id="time-alignment-and-look-ahead-bias">
<span id="factor-model-time-alignment"></span><h3>Time Alignment and Look-Ahead Bias<a class="headerlink" href="#time-alignment-and-look-ahead-bias" title="Link to this heading">#</a></h3>
<p>To avoid look-ahead bias from inconsistent time indexing and manual lagging across
characteristic fields, exposures and returns, skfolio uses a single as-of
time-indexing convention across all estimators, so data is aligned once and lags
enter as model parameters.</p>
<p>Under this convention, all time-varying inputs at observation <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(t\)</span> reflect
information available up to and including the end of period <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(t\)</span>. Point-in-time
fields and derived values (e.g. prices, fundamentals, industry labels and factor
exposures) store the latest available value for observation <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(t\)</span>. Returns
stored at observation <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(t\)</span> cover the period ending at <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(t\)</span>, namely
<span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\((t-1, t]\)</span>.</p>
<p>Factor-return regressions estimate the factor returns realized over
<span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\((t-1, t]\)</span>. The exposure matrix must therefore describe the assets before that
return interval begins, at <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(t-1\)</span>. <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">exposure_lag</span></code> selects that
exposure date and defaults to 1:</p>
<div class="math notranslate nohighlight">
\[R(t) = B(t - \ell)\,f(t) + \epsilon(t)\]</div>
<p>where <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(\ell\)</span> is <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">exposure_lag</span></code>. With the default <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(\ell = 1\)</span>, returns over
<span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\((t-1, t]\)</span> are regressed on exposures measured at <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(t-1\)</span>.
At each observation, this regression estimates realized factor returns and
idiosyncratic returns <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(\epsilon(t)\)</span>. Expected asset returns are subsequently
constructed from expected factor returns and, when configured, an alpha forecast.</p>
<p>The same alignment applies to regression weights. Market capitalization weights are
lagged by <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">exposure_lag</span></code>, and inverse-idiosyncratic-variance weights at date
<span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(t\)</span> are estimated from residuals up to <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(t-1\)</span>. Both are detailed in the
<a class="reference internal" href="#factor-model-regression-weights"><span class="std std-ref">Regression Weights</span></a> section.</p>
<p>Stored outputs follow the as-of time-indexing convention. <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">factor_model_.exposures</span></code> at <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(t\)</span>
stores the exposures measured at <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(t\)</span>, and the covariance forecast pairs the
factor covariance with the latest exposures <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(B(T)\)</span>. The lag is applied
internally when estimating realized factor returns and regression-based statistics.</p>
</section>
<section id="split-dividend-and-excess-return-conventions">
<h3>Split, Dividend and Excess Return Conventions<a class="headerlink" href="#split-dividend-and-excess-return-conventions" title="Link to this heading">#</a></h3>
<p>The <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">returns</span></code> field should contain total returns, computed from split and dividend
adjusted prices. It is the dependent variable of the cross-sectional regression and
the input to all return-based descriptors (beta, volatility, momentum, reversal).</p>
<p>Other market data used by descriptors, such as prices, trading volumes and shares
outstanding, should be split adjusted but not dividend adjusted. These enter
descriptors as price and quantity levels, for example as the denominator of a
valuation ratio or in turnover and liquidity measures, where reinvested dividends
would distort the level. The corresponding fields are detailed in the
<a class="reference internal" href="#factor-model-descriptor-input-fields"><span class="std std-ref">Descriptors</span></a> section. The <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">market_cap</span></code>
field should contain the market value of common equity at each date.</p>
<p>In a single-currency model, raw and excess returns give nearly identical regression
results, as subtracting a common risk-free rate shifts the cross-section by a constant
that the global factor absorbs. Excess returns remain preferable because
some descriptors estimate time-series regressions (e.g.
<a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.descriptor.EWMarketBeta.html#skfolio.descriptor.EWMarketBeta" title="skfolio.descriptor.EWMarketBeta"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">EWMarketBeta</span></code></a>,
<a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.descriptor.EWResidualVolatility.html#skfolio.descriptor.EWResidualVolatility" title="skfolio.descriptor.EWResidualVolatility"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">EWResidualVolatility</span></code></a>) whose time-series intercept should
capture residual return without absorbing the risk-free rate. In a multi-currency model,
<code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">returns</span></code> should contain local excess returns, and currency excess returns are
supplied through the <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">currency_excess_returns</span></code> argument. See the <a class="reference internal" href="#factor-model-currency-factors"><span class="std std-ref">Currency
Factors</span></a> section.</p>
</section>
</section>
<section id="factor-exposures">
<span id="factor-model-factor-exposures"></span><h2>Factor Exposures<a class="headerlink" href="#factor-exposures" title="Link to this heading">#</a></h2>
<p>Factor exposure estimators transform an <a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.containers.AssetPanel.html#skfolio.containers.AssetPanel" title="skfolio.containers.AssetPanel"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">AssetPanel</span></code></a> into
factor exposure arrays. Some estimators compute
exposures without descriptors, such as the global market factor or one-hot industry
factors. Others compute one or more descriptors and transform them into standardized
style exposures.</p>
<p>skfolio provides four factor exposure estimators:</p>
<ul class="simple">
<li><p><a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.factor_exposure.GlobalFactor.html#skfolio.factor_exposure.GlobalFactor" title="skfolio.factor_exposure.GlobalFactor"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">GlobalFactor</span></code></a> creates one common factor by
assigning every asset an exposure of 1.0. In the cross-sectional regression,
this factor acts as the cross-sectional regression intercept or broad market
factor. With the
benchmark-weighted centering and zero-sum constraints described in
<a class="reference internal" href="#factor-model-global-factor"><span class="std std-ref">Global Factor and Benchmark Portfolio</span></a>, its
estimated factor return captures the benchmark portfolio return.</p></li>
<li><p><a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.factor_exposure.OneHotCategoricalFactors.html#skfolio.factor_exposure.OneHotCategoricalFactors" title="skfolio.factor_exposure.OneHotCategoricalFactors"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">OneHotCategoricalFactors</span></code></a> turns a categorical
field into binary membership factors, one factor per category level. An asset has
exposure 1.0 to the factor matching its category and 0.0 to the other
category factors.</p></li>
<li><p><a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.factor_exposure.FixedWeightedFactor.html#skfolio.factor_exposure.FixedWeightedFactor" title="skfolio.factor_exposure.FixedWeightedFactor"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">FixedWeightedFactor</span></code></a> builds a factor from one or
more descriptors. Each descriptor is computed, passed through the outlier and
scoring transforms, and converted into a cross-sectional score. The descriptor
scores are then aggregated with the fixed descriptor weights. For each
asset-observation pair, non-finite scores are ignored and the weighted average is
divided by the weight assigned to the remaining finite scores, so an asset missing
a descriptor (e.g. gross margin for financial firms, which do not report cost of
goods sold) can still receive a composite score from its valid descriptors. The
<code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">min_coverage</span></code> parameter sets the minimum fraction of descriptor weight that must
be finite and when below this threshold, the composite is NaN. When multiple descriptors
are combined and scoring is enabled, the composite is scored again cross-sectionally
so partial-coverage composites remain on a comparable scale.</p></li>
<li><p><a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.factor_exposure.DerivedFactor.html#skfolio.factor_exposure.DerivedFactor" title="skfolio.factor_exposure.DerivedFactor"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">DerivedFactor</span></code></a> applies a function to another
factor’s exposure, then optionally applies outlier and scoring transforms. In
the <a class="reference internal" href="#factor-model-code-example"><span class="std std-ref">example</span></a>, it builds the non-linear size
factor from the size exposure (<code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">func=lambda</span> <span class="pre">x:</span> <span class="pre">x**3</span></code>). Dependencies between
factors are resolved automatically through topological sorting.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>Custom exposure estimators are created by subclassing
<a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.factor_exposure.BaseFactorExposure.html#skfolio.factor_exposure.BaseFactorExposure" title="skfolio.factor_exposure.BaseFactorExposure"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">BaseFactorExposure</span></code></a>.</p>
<p>Every factor exposure estimator carries a <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">family</span></code> attribute (e.g. <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">&quot;market&quot;</span></code>,
<code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">&quot;style&quot;</span></code>, <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">&quot;industry&quot;</span></code>, <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">&quot;country&quot;</span></code>, <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">&quot;currency&quot;</span></code>). Families group related factors and
are used in neutralization, zero-sum constraints, attribution and reporting.</p>
<section id="descriptors">
<span id="factor-model-descriptors"></span><h3>Descriptors<a class="headerlink" href="#descriptors" title="Link to this heading">#</a></h3>
<p>A descriptor is a transformer that reads one or more
<a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.containers.AssetPanel.html#skfolio.containers.AssetPanel" title="skfolio.containers.AssetPanel"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">AssetPanel</span></code></a> fields and returns a value array of shape
<code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">(n_observations,</span> <span class="pre">n_assets)</span></code>. Descriptors follow the as-of time-indexing convention: values at
observation <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(t\)</span> use information available up to and including the end of period
<span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(t\)</span>.</p>
<p>skfolio provides descriptors covering the standard factor literature:</p>
<div class="pst-scrollable-table-container"><table class="catalog-table table">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 20.0%" />
<col style="width: 80.0%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr class="row-odd"><th class="head"><p>Category</p></th>
<th class="head"><p>Descriptors</p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr class="row-even"><td><p>Value</p></td>
<td><ul class="simple">
<li><p><a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.descriptor.BookToPrice.html#skfolio.descriptor.BookToPrice" title="skfolio.descriptor.BookToPrice"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">BookToPrice</span></code></a></p></li>
<li><p><a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.descriptor.CashFlowToPrice.html#skfolio.descriptor.CashFlowToPrice" title="skfolio.descriptor.CashFlowToPrice"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">CashFlowToPrice</span></code></a></p></li>
<li><p><a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.descriptor.SalesToPrice.html#skfolio.descriptor.SalesToPrice" title="skfolio.descriptor.SalesToPrice"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">SalesToPrice</span></code></a></p></li>

<tr class="row-odd"><td><p>Earnings yield</p></td>

<li><p><a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.descriptor.EbitdaToEnterpriseValue.html#skfolio.descriptor.EbitdaToEnterpriseValue" title="skfolio.descriptor.EbitdaToEnterpriseValue"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">EbitdaToEnterpriseValue</span></code></a></p></li>
</ul>
</td>
</tr>
<tr class="row-even"><td><p>Growth</p></td>
<td><ul class="simple">
<li><p><a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.descriptor.AssetsGrowthRate.html#skfolio.descriptor.AssetsGrowthRate" title="skfolio.descriptor.AssetsGrowthRate"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">AssetsGrowthRate</span></code></a></p></li>

<p><a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.descriptor.Passthrough.html#skfolio.descriptor.Passthrough" title="skfolio.descriptor.Passthrough"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">Passthrough</span></code></a> exposes an existing panel field
as a descriptor without transformation, which is useful for vendor-supplied or
externally computed values. Custom descriptors are created by subclassing
<a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.descriptor.BaseDescriptor.html#skfolio.descriptor.BaseDescriptor" title="skfolio.descriptor.BaseDescriptor"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">BaseDescriptor</span></code></a>.</p>
