<p>The <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">adj_</span></code> fields should be split adjusted but not dividend adjusted, since descriptors use them as price and
quantity levels where reinvested dividends would distort the level. The per-share estimate fields <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">eps_ntm</span></code>,
<code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">dps_ntm</span></code> and <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">eps_ntm_std</span></code> must share the same split-adjustment basis as <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">adj_close</span></code>,
so that per-share ratios such as forward earnings yield are consistent.</p>
</section>
<section id="cross-sectional-transformers">
<span id="factor-model-cross-sectional-transformers"></span><h3>Cross-Sectional Transformers<a class="headerlink" href="#cross-sectional-transformers" title="Link to this heading">#</a></h3>
<p>Raw descriptor values have heavy tails and incomparable units (a book-to-price ratio
and a turnover rate are on different scales). Each descriptor is therefore passed
through two cross-sectional transformers before being combined:</p>
<ul class="simple">
<li><p><code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">outlier_transformer</span></code>, defaulting to
<a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.preprocessing.CSWinsorizer.html#skfolio.preprocessing.CSWinsorizer" title="skfolio.preprocessing.CSWinsorizer"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">CSWinsorizer</span></code></a>, caps extreme values.</p></li>
<li><p><code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">scoring_transformer</span></code>, defaulting to
<a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.preprocessing.CSStandardScaler.html#skfolio.preprocessing.CSStandardScaler" title="skfolio.preprocessing.CSStandardScaler"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">CSStandardScaler</span></code></a>, converts values into
cross-sectional z-scores.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>With the default scoring, each descriptor is centered on the benchmark-weighted
mean (market-cap-weighted by default) and scaled by the equal-weighted standard
deviation. Benchmark-weighted centering gives the benchmark portfolio zero exposure
to every style factor. Equal-weighted scaling keeps the dispersion estimate from
being dominated by the largest assets.</p>
<p>An exposure of 1.0 means the asset is one standard deviation above the
benchmark-weighted average. The zero benchmark exposure determines the
interpretation of the global factor return, described in
<a class="reference internal" href="#factor-model-global-factor"><span class="std std-ref">Global Factor and Benchmark Portfolio</span></a>.
Transform statistics are computed on the estimation universe and applied to the
full coverage universe.</p>
<p>The <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">transform_by_group</span></code> parameter applies both transforms within groups defined by a
categorical panel field (e.g. “industry”, “country”). For example, with
<code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">transform_by_group=&quot;industry&quot;</span></code>, each descriptor is scored within
its own industry group, making cross-sectional scores more comparable across industries.
This is useful because book-to-price, profitability, leverage, and similar descriptors
can have very different distributions across industries. Group-level scoring also makes
the resulting factor industry-neutral (see <a class="reference internal" href="#factor-model-neutralization"><span class="std std-ref">Neutralization</span></a>)
and prevents a single industry from dominating the factor’s variation.</p>
<p>The following transformers are available:</p>
<p><strong>Outlier transformers</strong></p>
<ul class="simple">
<li><p><a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.preprocessing.CSWinsorizer.html#skfolio.preprocessing.CSWinsorizer" title="skfolio.preprocessing.CSWinsorizer"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">CSWinsorizer</span></code></a></p></li>
<li><p><a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.preprocessing.CSTanhShrinker.html#skfolio.preprocessing.CSTanhShrinker" title="skfolio.preprocessing.CSTanhShrinker"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">CSTanhShrinker</span></code></a></p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Scoring transformers</strong></p>
<ul class="simple">
<li><p><a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.preprocessing.CSStandardScaler.html#skfolio.preprocessing.CSStandardScaler" title="skfolio.preprocessing.CSStandardScaler"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">CSStandardScaler</span></code></a></p></li>
<li><p><a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.preprocessing.CSGaussianRankScaler.html#skfolio.preprocessing.CSGaussianRankScaler" title="skfolio.preprocessing.CSGaussianRankScaler"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">CSGaussianRankScaler</span></code></a></p></li>
<li><p><a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.preprocessing.CSPercentileRankScaler.html#skfolio.preprocessing.CSPercentileRankScaler" title="skfolio.preprocessing.CSPercentileRankScaler"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">CSPercentileRankScaler</span></code></a></p></li>
</ul>
<p>See <a class="reference internal" href="cross_sectional_transformers.html#cross-sectional-transformers"><span class="std std-ref">Cross-Sectional Transformers</span></a> for details.</p>
</section>
<section id="neutralization">
<span id="factor-model-neutralization"></span><h3>Neutralization<a class="headerlink" href="#neutralization" title="Link to this heading">#</a></h3>
<p>Style exposures are often correlated with other factors. For example, volatility
correlates with beta, and an un-neutralized value exposure can carry industry tilts.
Neutralization (also called orthogonalization) removes these overlaps, producing “pure”
factor definitions, reducing collinearity in the cross-sectional regression and making
factor returns easier to interpret.</p>
<p>The <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">neutralize_against</span></code> parameter maps each factor (or family) to the target
factors or families. The <a class="reference internal" href="#factor-model-code-example"><span class="std std-ref">example</span></a> uses:</p>
<div class="highlight-python notranslate"><div class="highlight"><pre><span></span><span class="n">neutralize_against</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="p">{</span>
    <span class="s2">"non_linear_size"</span><span class="p">:</span> <span class="p">[</span><span class="s2">"size"</span><span class="p">],</span>
    <span class="s2">"volatility"</span><span class="p">:</span> <span class="p">[</span><span class="s2">"beta"</span><span class="p">],</span>
<span class="p">}</span>
</pre></div>
</div>
<p>Neutralization is a weighted least squares projection. For a style exposure <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(z\)</span>
and target exposures <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(D\)</span> under benchmark weights <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(W\)</span>:</p>
<div class="math notranslate nohighlight">
\[z^{\perp} = z - D\,(D^\top W D)^{-1}\,D^\top W z\]</div>
<p>The residual <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(z^{\perp}\)</span> is orthogonal to the target factors under the
benchmark-weighted inner product and is re-standardized afterwards. Entries are
processed in insertion order such that later entries operate on exposures already
modified by earlier ones.</p>
<p>When the target factors form a one-hot categorical family, the projection reduces
to demeaning within each group. Taking industry as an example, neutralizing a
style against “industry” through <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">neutralize_against</span></code> and scoring it within
industries through <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">transform_by_group=&quot;industry&quot;</span></code> will produce the same neutrality
because the exposure has zero benchmark-weighted mean within every industry
(<span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(D^\top W z = 0\)</span>). The resulting exposures differ only in per-industry
scaling: the projection removes the per-industry mean, while within-industry
scoring also divides each industry by its own standard deviation, keeping the
factor from being dominated by the industry with the largest spread in the raw
descriptor. For one-hot target factors, <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">transform_by_group</span></code> is preferred as it both
normalizes per-industry scale and is cheaper (group demeaning instead of a full
projection). When both are applied, the projection has no effect because the
exposure already satisfies the same orthogonality condition.</p>
</section>
<section id="exposure-diagnostics">
<span id="factor-model-diagnostics"></span><h3>Exposure Diagnostics<a class="headerlink" href="#exposure-diagnostics" title="Link to this heading">#</a></h3>
<p>Exposure diagnostics assess the stability and conditioning of the factor
exposure matrix. Strong collinearity between exposures inflates the variance of
the estimated factor returns and can make attribution unstable.
<a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.prior.FactorModel.html#skfolio.prior.FactorModel.exposure_correlation" title="skfolio.prior.FactorModel.exposure_correlation"><code class="xref py py-meth docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">exposure_correlation</span></code></a> reports the
time-average pairwise correlation of exposures,
<a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.prior.FactorModel.html#skfolio.prior.FactorModel.exposure_vif" title="skfolio.prior.FactorModel.exposure_vif"><code class="xref py py-attr docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">exposure_vif</span></code></a> the per-factor variance inflation
factors and <a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.prior.FactorModel.html#skfolio.prior.FactorModel.exposure_condition_number" title="skfolio.prior.FactorModel.exposure_condition_number"><code class="xref py py-attr docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">exposure_condition_number</span></code></a> the
conditioning of the regression design. When zero-sum constraints are active, VIF
and condition number are computed in the reduced full-rank basis.</p>
<p><code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">exposure_correlation</span></code> accepts a <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">cs_weighting</span></code> parameter. The default
<code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">&quot;benchmark&quot;</span></code> measures orthogonality in the metric used by neutralization,
<code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">&quot;identity&quot;</span></code> (equal weighting) uses a different inner product and can show a
residual correlation of 0.1 to 0.3 even when the factor is exactly
benchmark-neutral, and <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">&quot;regression&quot;</span></code> measures multicollinearity as seen by the
WLS regression.</p>
<div class="highlight-python notranslate"><div class="highlight"><pre><span></span><span class="n">factor_model</span><span class="o">.</span><span class="n">plot_exposure_correlation</span><span class="p">(</span><span class="n">families</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="p">[</span><span class="s2">"market"</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="s2">"style"</span><span class="p">])</span>
</pre></div>
</div>
<figure class="factor-model-figure">
  <img src="../_static/factor_model/plots/factor_model_exposure_correlation.webp"
       alt="Time-average correlation heatmap for market and style factor exposures"
       width="1100" height="616"
       loading="lazy" decoding="async"
       style="display:block;width:100%;height:auto;">
</figure><p>In the <a class="reference internal" href="#factor-model-code-example"><span class="std std-ref">example</span></a>, the market exposure is constant
(every asset has unit exposure), so its correlation row is uninformative and
displays as zero. Market neutrality is instead guaranteed by benchmark-weighted
centering: every exposure has a zero benchmark-weighted mean, so no factor
carries net market exposure. The volatility-beta correlation and the correlation
between non-linear size and size are zero, as expected from the neutralization.
The remaining correlations are moderate, indicating no redundant factors. The
<code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">families</span></code> argument excludes the 44 industry factors for readability. When
included, their correlations with the style factors are zero as well, the result
of within-industry scoring through <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">transform_by_group=&quot;industry&quot;</span></code>, and the
industry-industry correlations are slightly negative rather than zero. Each asset
belongs to exactly one industry, so membership in one industry rules out
membership in all others. Zero correlation would mean industry memberships are
independent, while this exclusion is a negative relationship.</p>
<p><a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.prior.FactorModel.html#skfolio.prior.FactorModel.plot_exposure_stability" title="skfolio.prior.FactorModel.plot_exposure_stability"><code class="xref py py-meth docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">plot_exposure_stability</span></code></a> shows the
cross-sectional correlation of each factor’s exposures between observations
with <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">step</span></code> determining how far apart the observations are sampled (21 by default).
Slow-moving factors (e.g. value, size) should stay highly correlated at a monthly
step. Fast-turnover factors (e.g. reversal, short-term momentum) reshuffle quickly
by construction and naturally show lower stability at that horizon. It is therefore
recommended to assess these factors with a shorter <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">step</span></code> (e.g. 1 to 5 days).</p>
<div class="highlight-python notranslate"><div class="highlight"><pre><span></span><span class="n">factor_model</span><span class="o">.</span><span class="n">plot_exposure_stability</span><span class="p">(</span><span class="n">families</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="p">[</span><span class="s2">"market"</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="s2">"style"</span><span class="p">])</span>
</pre></div>
</div>
<figure class="factor-model-figure">
  <img src="../_static/factor_model/plots/factor_model_exposure_stability.webp"
       alt="Monthly exposure stability through time for market and style factors"
       width="1100" height="616"
       loading="lazy" decoding="async"
       style="display:block;width:100%;height:auto;">
</figure><p>In the <a class="reference internal" href="#factor-model-code-example"><span class="std std-ref">example</span></a>, monthly stability stays above
0.8 for nearly all factors and dates, with short-lived dips during stress
episodes (e.g. March 2020). Stable exposures keep the risk decomposition
consistent between rebalancings and limit the turnover induced by exposure noise.</p>
<p><a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.prior.FactorModel.html#skfolio.prior.FactorModel" title="skfolio.prior.FactorModel"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">FactorModel</span></code></a> provides additional exposure diagnostics:</p>
<ul class="simple">
<li><p><a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.prior.FactorModel.html#skfolio.prior.FactorModel.plot_exposure_vif" title="skfolio.prior.FactorModel.plot_exposure_vif"><code class="xref py py-meth docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">plot_exposure_vif</span></code></a></p></li>
<li><p><a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.prior.FactorModel.html#skfolio.prior.FactorModel.plot_exposure_condition_number" title="skfolio.prior.FactorModel.plot_exposure_condition_number"><code class="xref py py-meth docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">plot_exposure_condition_number</span></code></a></p></li>
<li><p><a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.prior.FactorModel.html#skfolio.prior.FactorModel.plot_exposure_distribution" title="skfolio.prior.FactorModel.plot_exposure_distribution"><code class="xref py py-meth docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">plot_exposure_distribution</span></code></a></p></li>
<li><p><a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.prior.FactorModel.html#skfolio.prior.FactorModel.plot_exposure_dispersion" title="skfolio.prior.FactorModel.plot_exposure_dispersion"><code class="xref py py-meth docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">plot_exposure_dispersion</span></code></a></p></li>
</ul>
</section>
</section>
<section id="cross-sectional-regression">
<span id="factor-model-cross-sectional-regression"></span><h2>Cross-Sectional Regression<a class="headerlink" href="#cross-sectional-regression" title="Link to this heading">#</a></h2>
<p>After factor exposures are computed, the cross-sectional regression uses the
exposure tensor of shape <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">(n_observations,</span> <span class="pre">n_assets,</span> <span class="pre">n_factors)</span></code> together with
the asset return matrix of shape <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">(n_observations,</span> <span class="pre">n_assets)</span></code>. For each
observation, the model estimates factor returns and idiosyncratic returns by
regressing the cross-section of asset returns on the corresponding lagged exposures.</p>
<section id="regression-model">
<h3>Regression Model<a class="headerlink" href="#regression-model" title="Link to this heading">#</a></h3>
<p>For each observation <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(t\)</span>, factor returns solve the weighted least squares
problem:</p>
<div class="math notranslate nohighlight">
\[\hat{f}(t) = \arg\min_{f} \sum_{i} w_i(t)\,
\big(R_i(t) - B_i(t - \ell)^\top f\big)^2\]</div>
<p>where <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(w_i(t)\)</span> are the weights described in
<a class="reference internal" href="#factor-model-regression-weights"><span class="std std-ref">Regression Weights</span></a>,
<span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(B_i(t - \ell)\)</span> is asset <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(i\)</span>’s lagged exposure vector and <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(\ell\)</span> is
<code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">exposure_lag</span></code>. Idiosyncratic returns are obtained as the regression residuals:</p>
<div class="math notranslate nohighlight">
\[\hat{\epsilon}_i(t) = R_i(t) - B_i(t - \ell)^\top \hat{f}(t)\]</div>
<p>The regression is performed by <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">cs_regressor</span></code>, defaulting to
<a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.linear_model.CSLinearRegression.html#skfolio.linear_model.CSLinearRegression" title="skfolio.linear_model.CSLinearRegression"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">CSLinearRegression</span></code></a>, a weighted least-squares
estimator that solves all observations in one vectorized pass over the exposure
tensor. For robust or regularized cross-sectional estimation, skfolio allows
scikit-learn-compatible estimators (e.g. <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">HuberRegressor</span></code> for outlier-robust
regression) to be passed through
<a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.linear_model.CSLinearRegressorWrapper.html#skfolio.linear_model.CSLinearRegressorWrapper" title="skfolio.linear_model.CSLinearRegressorWrapper"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">CSLinearRegressorWrapper</span></code></a>, which applies the
wrapped estimator separately to each observation.</p>
<div class="admonition note">
<p class="admonition-title">Note</p>
<p>Unlike factor exposures, which are typically winsorized and standardized by
the exposure estimators, asset returns enter the cross-sectional regression
unadjusted. Cleaning return data errors is an upstream responsibility, since
the same returns drive benchmark weights, realized performance and downstream
optimization. Winsorizing legitimate extreme returns would break the
reconciliation of <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(R = B\,f + \epsilon\)</span> and understate idiosyncratic
risk for heavy-tailed assets. Extreme observations are already moderated by
regression weights (<code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">regression_mcap_power</span></code> and <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">inv_idio_variance_weight_shrinkage</span></code>).
When the factor-return regression needs to further reduce the influence of extreme
asset returns, use a robust regressor such as <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">HuberRegressor</span></code> through
<a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.linear_model.CSLinearRegressorWrapper.html#skfolio.linear_model.CSLinearRegressorWrapper" title="skfolio.linear_model.CSLinearRegressorWrapper"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">CSLinearRegressorWrapper</span></code></a> as described above.</p>
</div>
</section>
<section id="regression-weights">
<span id="factor-model-regression-weights"></span><h3>Regression Weights<a class="headerlink" href="#regression-weights" title="Link to this heading">#</a></h3>
<p>Idiosyncratic returns are heteroskedastic, meaning idiosyncratic variance differs widely
across the cross-section. An unweighted regression would give excessive influence to the
noisiest names. Under heteroskedasticity, the best linear unbiased estimator (BLUE) is the
weighted least squares regression whose weights are proportional to inverse idiosyncratic
variance, known as generalized least squares (GLS).</p>
<p>Idiosyncratic variances are not observable before the regression is run. It is common practice
to approximate the inverse-variance weights with square-root market-cap weights (empirically,
idiosyncratic variance decreases roughly as the inverse square root of market capitalization).
The <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">regression_mcap_power</span></code> parameter controls this weighting as a power
<span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(p\)</span> of market capitalization:</p>
<div class="math notranslate nohighlight">
\[w_i \propto \mathrm{mcap}_i^{\,p}\]</div>
<p>with <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">0.5</span></code> (default) for square-root cap weighting, <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">0.0</span></code> for equal weighting and
<code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">1.0</span></code> for cap weighting. Market cap moves with the return being regressed,
<span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(\mathrm{mcap}_i(t) \approx \mathrm{mcap}_i(t-1)\,(1 + R_i(t))\)</span>, and
weighting the regression at <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(t\)</span> by caps at <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(t\)</span> would correlate the
weights with the regressed returns and bias the estimated factor returns.
Because of this, market caps are also lagged by <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">exposure_lag</span></code>.</p>
<p>The model also supports inverse-idiosyncratic-variance weighting with a two-step
feasible GLS. A first pass runs with the cap-based weights and then the variances
estimated from its residuals (up to <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(t - 1\)</span> only) feed the weights of the
second pass. The regression weights never depend on their own output, avoiding
the feedback loop of recursive weighting schemes where a low estimated variance
increases an asset’s weight and, in turn, its influence on later residuals.
<code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">inv_idio_variance_max_weight_ratio</span></code> (default 20) caps each inverse-variance
weight at a multiple of the cross-sectional median, preventing assets with very
low estimated variance from dominating the regression.</p>
<p>Estimated variances are noisy, and <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">inv_idio_variance_weight_shrinkage</span></code> blends
the two weightings for robustness:</p>
<div class="math notranslate nohighlight">
\[w_i = \lambda\,w_i^{\text{inv-var}} + (1 - \lambda)\,w_i^{\text{cap}}\]</div>
<p>where <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(\lambda = 0\)</span> (default) uses the cap-based weights only and
<span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(\lambda = 1\)</span> the inverse-variance weights only.</p>
<p>The weights used at each date are stored in <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">factor_model_.regression_weights</span></code>: row
<span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(t\)</span> holds the weights used by the regression at <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(t\)</span>, built from market
caps at <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(t - \ell\)</span> and idiosyncratic variances estimated up to <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(t - 1\)</span>.</p>
</section>
<section id="zero-sum-constraints">
<span id="factor-model-zero-sum-constraints"></span><h3>Zero-Sum Constraints<a class="headerlink" href="#zero-sum-constraints" title="Link to this heading">#</a></h3>
<p>One-hot factor families are exactly collinear with the global factor: each
asset’s industry exposures sum to one, which is the global exposure. Including
both leaves the factor returns unidentified as adding a constant to every industry
return and subtracting it from the global return leaves the fit unchanged, and
the exposure design is rank-deficient.</p>
<p>The <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">constrained_families</span></code> parameter resolves this by imposing a benchmark-weighted
zero-sum constraint on the factor returns of each constrained family. Economically,
the global factor captures the benchmark portfolio return and the constrained family
factors capture relative effects around it.</p>
<p>Each tuple <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">(family,</span> <span class="pre">factor_to_drop)</span></code> specifies a family to reparameterize.
Instead of solving a constrained regression, the model applies an equivalent
change of basis. For a constrained family with exposure columns <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(x_j\)</span> and
factor returns <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(\beta_j\)</span>, the constraint reads
<span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(\sum_j w_j \beta_j = 0\)</span>, where <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(w_j\)</span> is the benchmark weight
aggregated per factor (for one-hot industries, the benchmark weight of each
industry). Solving the constraint for one factor <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(k\)</span> and substituting into
the regression yields an unconstrained regression on transformed features
<span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(z_j\)</span>, with the dropped factor’s return reconstructed from the constraint
after fitting:</p>
<div class="math notranslate nohighlight">
\[z_j = x_j - \frac{w_j}{w_k}\,x_k,
\qquad
\hat{\beta}_k = -\frac{1}{w_k} \sum_{j \neq k} w_j \hat{\beta}_j\]</div>
<p>The full family is reported with no loss of information, and all downstream
computations such as the factor covariance estimator and the
<a class="reference internal" href="#factor-model-regression-diagnostics"><span class="std std-ref">regression diagnostics</span></a> (t-statistics,
VIF, condition number, adjusted <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(R^2\)</span>) operate on a full-rank design.
Any choice of <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(k\)</span> yields the same constrained solution. When <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">factor_to_drop</span></code> is <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">None</span></code>,
the model drops the factor with the largest <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(|w_k|\)</span>, keeping the ratios
<span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(|w_j / w_k|\)</span> bounded by 1.0 for most factors and preserving the
conditioning of the reduced design. The basis is stored in
<code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">factor_model_.family_constraint_basis</span></code> and follows the same timing as the
regression. Realized factor returns are reconstructed with the lagged ratios used
by the regression, while expected factor returns and covariance are expanded with
the latest as-of basis.</p>
</section>
<section id="global-factor-and-benchmark-portfolio">
<span id="factor-model-global-factor"></span><h3>Global Factor and Benchmark Portfolio<a class="headerlink" href="#global-factor-and-benchmark-portfolio" title="Link to this heading">#</a></h3>
<p>Under the exposure centering and zero-sum constraints described in
<a class="reference internal" href="#factor-model-zero-sum-constraints"><span class="std std-ref">Zero-Sum Constraints</span></a>, the global
factor return captures the benchmark portfolio return. Aggregating the
cross-sectional regression over the assets <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(i\)</span> of the estimation
universe under benchmark weights gives (time indices omitted):</p>
<div class="math notranslate nohighlight">
\[\underbrace{\sum_i w_i^{\text{bench}} R_i}_{\text{benchmark return}}
= \hat{f}_0
+ \underbrace{\sum_{j \in \text{industry, country}} w_j\,\hat{f}_j}_{=\,0
\text{ (zero-sum constraint)}}
+ \sum_{j \in \text{styles}} \underbrace{\Big(\sum_i w_i^{\text{bench}}
B_{ij}\Big)}_{=\,0 \text{ (benchmark-centered)}} \hat{f}_j
+ \underbrace{\sum_i w_i^{\text{bench}}\,\hat{\epsilon}_i}_{\approx\,0}\]</div>
<p>where <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(\hat{f}_0\)</span> is the global factor return, <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(\hat{f}_j\)</span> the
other factor returns, <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(B_{ij}\)</span> the exposure of asset <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(i\)</span> to factor
<span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(j\)</span> and <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(\hat{\epsilon}_i\)</span> its idiosyncratic return.</p>
<ul class="simple">
<li><p>For a constrained one-hot family such as industry or country, the benchmark
exposure to factor <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(j\)</span> is the total benchmark weight of the assets in
that category, <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(w_j = \sum_i w_i^{\text{bench}} B_{ij}\)</span> (the industry
cap share when <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">benchmark_mcap_power=1</span></code>). The family term
<span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(\sum_j w_j \hat{f}_j\)</span> is the benchmark-weighted average of the
family’s factor returns, set to zero by the zero-sum constraint.</p></li>
<li><p>Style exposures are centered so that the benchmark-weighted average
exposure <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(\sum_i w_i^{\text{bench}} B_{ij}\)</span> is zero (the default
<a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.preprocessing.CSStandardScaler.html#skfolio.preprocessing.CSStandardScaler" title="skfolio.preprocessing.CSStandardScaler"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">CSStandardScaler</span></code></a> behavior). The benchmark
has no style tilt and style factor returns do not contribute to its
return.</p></li>
<li><p>The residual term is the benchmark-weighted average of the idiosyncratic
returns. The weighted residuals sum to zero under the regression weights,
and the remaining difference between benchmark and regression weights is
diversified across the cross-section.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>The estimated global factor return tracks the benchmark portfolio return on
the estimation universe (e.g. the market-cap portfolio when
<code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">benchmark_mcap_power=1</span></code>), with deviations limited to the residual term when
regression weights differ from benchmark weights. When
<code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">regression_mcap_power</span> <span class="pre">==</span> <span class="pre">benchmark_mcap_power</span></code> and
<code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">inv_idio_variance_weight_shrinkage</span> <span class="pre">==</span> <span class="pre">0</span></code>, the regression weights are
proportional to the benchmark weights and the residual term is exactly zero:
the WLS normal equations make the weighted residuals orthogonal to every
column of the exposure design, including the global column of ones, so
<span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(\sum_i w_i^{\text{bench}} \hat{\epsilon}_i = 0\)</span> and the identity is
exact:</p>
<div class="math notranslate nohighlight">
\[\hat{f}_0(t) = \sum_i w_i^{\text{bench}}\,R_i(t)\]</div>
<p>The benchmark weights are stored in <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">factor_model_.benchmark_weights</span></code>. Under this
structure, each factor return reads as a portfolio return relative to the
benchmark. The global factor is the benchmark return itself. An industry factor
return is a benchmark-relative industry effect meaning it captures the return earned by that industry
in excess of the benchmark, net of the other factors. A style factor return is the
return to a standardized characteristic tilt, representing the return earned by holding one
standard deviation of exposure to that characteristic while keeping all other
factor exposures at zero.</p>
</section>
<section id="missing-data-and-changing-universes">
<span id="factor-model-missing-data"></span><h3>Missing Data and Changing Universes<a class="headerlink" href="#missing-data-and-changing-universes" title="Link to this heading">#</a></h3>
<p>Because the regression is re-estimated independently at each date, changing universes
are handled naturally, with newly listed assets joining the regression as soon as their
exposures are available, delisted assets dropping out, and assets on holiday
excluded for that date only. No realignment or imputation is needed.</p>
<p>At each date, an asset participates in the regression when it belongs to the
estimation universe and its lagged exposures and return are finite. All other pairs
receive zero regression weight. Assets outside the estimation universe still receive
exposures, idiosyncratic returns and forecasts where computable.</p>
<p>As a guard against underspecified regressions, <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">min_regression_assets</span></code> sets the
minimum number of participating assets required at every observation after the
<a class="reference internal" href="#factor-model-warmup"><span class="std std-ref">warmup period</span></a> (default
<code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">max(2</span> <span class="pre">*</span> <span class="pre">n_factors,</span> <span class="pre">30)</span></code>). A ValueError is raised when a cross-section falls below
this minimum.</p>
</section>
<section id="currency-factors">
<span id="factor-model-currency-factors"></span><h3>Currency Factors<a class="headerlink" href="#currency-factors" title="Link to this heading">#</a></h3>
<p>In a multi-currency universe, asset returns in the investor’s numeraire mix equity
risk with currency risk. The model separates the two components by estimating non-currency
factors from local excess returns and adding currency risk through dedicated currency factors.</p>
<p>The <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">currency_factor</span></code> parameter takes a one-hot exposure estimator on the asset currency
field (typically <a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.factor_exposure.OneHotCategoricalFactors.html#skfolio.factor_exposure.OneHotCategoricalFactors" title="skfolio.factor_exposure.OneHotCategoricalFactors"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">OneHotCategoricalFactors</span></code></a>),
and the <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">currency_excess_returns</span></code> argument of <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">fit</span></code> supplies the currency excess return
series. The base-currency excess return of asset <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(i\)</span> decomposes as:</p>
<div class="math notranslate nohighlight">
\[R^{excess,base}_i(t) = R^{excess,local}_i(t) + R^{ccy}_{C_i(t)}(t)\]</div>
<p>where <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(R^{excess,local}_i(t)\)</span> is the asset’s local-currency return in
excess of the cash rate of its currency <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(C_i(t)\)</span> and
<span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(R^{ccy}_{C_i(t)}(t)\)</span> is the currency excess return from converting that
local-currency asset return into the investor’s base currency:</p>
<div class="math notranslate nohighlight">
\[R^{ccy}_{C_i(t)}(t) = R^{FX}_{C_i(t)}(t) + r^{cash}_{C_i(t)}(t)
- r^{cash}_{base}(t) + R^{local}_i(t)\,R^{FX}_{C_i(t)}(t)\]</div>
<p>The identity follows from compounding the local return with the FX return and
subtracting the cash rates defining each excess return (time indices omitted):</p>
<div class="math notranslate nohighlight">
\[\begin{split}R^{base}_i &amp;= (1 + R^{local}_i)(1 + R^{FX}_{C_i}) - 1
= R^{local}_i + R^{FX}_{C_i} + R^{local}_i\,R^{FX}_{C_i} \\
R^{excess,base}_i &amp;= R^{base}_i - r^{cash}_{base} \\
&amp;= \big(R^{local}_i - r^{cash}_{C_i}\big)
+ R^{FX}_{C_i} + r^{cash}_{C_i} - r^{cash}_{base}
+ R^{local}_i\,R^{FX}_{C_i} \\
&amp;= R^{excess,local}_i + R^{ccy}_{C_i}\end{split}\]</div>
<p>The currency excess return series are computed by the user and supplied through
<code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">currency_excess_returns</span></code>, with one column per currency factor. Values must be finite
for the fitted observations. Leading rows consumed by descriptor warmup and exposure lag
are not used and may contain NaN, such as the first row of a series computed with
<code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">pct_change</span></code>.</p>
<p>Unlike equity factor returns, currency factor returns are not estimated by the
regression but instead are observed FX series in the investor’s numeraire which are then
appended directly to the factor return distribution with family <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">&quot;currency&quot;</span></code>. The factor
covariance then captures both equity and currency factor risk, and each asset loads
on its currency through the one-hot exposures.</p>
</section>
<section id="regression-diagnostics">
<span id="factor-model-regression-diagnostics"></span><h3>Regression Diagnostics<a class="headerlink" href="#regression-diagnostics" title="Link to this heading">#</a></h3>

</div><p>In the <a class="reference internal" href="#factor-model-code-example"><span class="std std-ref">example</span></a>, momentum carries the highest
annualized Sharpe ratio (0.62) and the largest cumulative return, with the sharp
2020 reversal characteristic of momentum crashes. The beta, size, earnings yield
and profitability factors show positive premia over the sample while value is
flat. The mean VIFs are all below 5, consistent with the moderate exposure
correlations observed in <a class="reference internal" href="#factor-model-diagnostics"><span class="std std-ref">Exposure Diagnostics</span></a>.</p>
<div class="admonition note">
<p class="admonition-title">Note</p>
<p>These are pure-factor returns, not sorted long-short factor portfolio returns
such as the Fama-French factors. Their numerical scale is generally smaller
than that of familiar academic factor portfolios. Each factor return can be
interpreted as the return of a factor-mimicking portfolio constructed by the
cross-sectional regression. This portfolio is not rescaled to a fixed gross
exposure or volatility, whereas academic factors are often constructed as
100% long and 100% short (200% gross exposure) and may carry several units
of factor exposure together with incidental exposures to other factors.
Factor-return series should therefore only be compared after matching
exposure scaling and portfolio normalization conventions. Sharpe ratios are
more comparable because rescaling changes the mean and volatility proportionally.</p>
<p>In a characteristics factor model, each factor return is the cross-sectional
regression coefficient for one unit of exposure. For style factors built from
standardized exposures, this corresponds to one cross-sectional standard
deviation of exposure after winsorization, within-industry scoring and
neutralization. Equivalently, the factor-mimicking portfolio has unit
exposure to that factor and zero exposure to the other factors.</p>
<p>Factor-return signs follow the exposure convention. The size factor is built
from <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">LogMarketCap</span></code>, so a positive size factor return means large-cap
exposure was rewarded, the opposite of the Fama-French small-minus-big
convention. The sign convention only matters when comparing to external
factor series and it does not affect covariance decomposition, attribution or
optimization because exposures and factor returns are used consistently
within the model.</p>
</div>
<p><a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.prior.FactorModel.html#skfolio.prior.FactorModel.cs_regression_scores" title="skfolio.prior.FactorModel.cs_regression_scores"><code class="xref py py-attr docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">cs_regression_scores</span></code></a> returns per-observation
fit statistics: <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">r2</span></code>, <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">adjusted_r2</span></code> (adjusted for the effective number of
regressors, reduced when constraints are active), <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">aic</span></code> and <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">bic</span></code>.</p>
<div class="highlight-python notranslate"><div class="highlight"><pre><span></span><span class="n">factor_model</span><span class="o">.</span><span class="n">cs_regression_scores</span><span class="o">.</span><span class="n">mean</span><span class="p">()</span>
</pre></div>
</div>
<div class="highlight-text notranslate"><div class="highlight"><pre><span></span>r2                 0.312856
adjusted_r2        0.278087
aic           -10183.548079
bic            -9894.130723
</pre></div>
</div>
<p><a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.prior.FactorModel.html#skfolio.prior.FactorModel.plot_cs_regression_scores" title="skfolio.prior.FactorModel.plot_cs_regression_scores"><code class="xref py py-meth docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">plot_cs_regression_scores</span></code></a> displays them
through time:</p>
<div class="highlight-python notranslate"><div class="highlight"><pre><span></span><span class="n">factor_model</span><span class="o">.</span><span class="n">plot_cs_regression_scores</span><span class="p">(</span><span class="n">score</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="s2">"adjusted_r2"</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="n">window</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="mi">20</span><span class="p">)</span>
</pre></div>
</div>
<figure class="factor-model-figure">
  <img src="../_static/factor_model/plots/factor_model_cs_regression_scores_adjusted_r2.webp"
       alt="Rolling 20-day adjusted R-squared of the daily cross-sectional factor regressions"
       width="1100" height="616"
       loading="lazy" decoding="async"
       style="display:block;width:100%;height:auto;">
</figure><p>The mean <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(R^2\)</span> is an in-sample quantity and does not account for model
complexity or overfitting, making it a weak measure of model quality. It does however
remain useful as a sanity check. For daily US equity models, the mean <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(R^2\)</span> typically
falls between 25% and 40% and the mean adjusted <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(R^2\)</span> between 20% and 35%.
Values outside these ranges typically warrant investigation. Three caveats apply:</p>
<ul class="simple">
<li><p>A mean <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(R^2\)</span> of 30% does not mean the model explains only 30% of portfolio risk:
the daily cross-sectional <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(R^2\)</span> is an in-sample fit statistic for that
day’s stock returns, while a portfolio’s risk can still be dominated by common
factors because idiosyncratic terms diversify away and factor terms do not.</p></li>
<li><p>Some vendors report <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(R^2\)</span> on monthly returns, which is mechanically higher than
on daily returns, and figures are only comparable at the same frequency.</p></li>
<li><p><span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(R^2\)</span> should not be used to compare different models as the addition of any
factor will lead to a larger value.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.prior.FactorModel.html#skfolio.prior.FactorModel.cs_regression_t_stats" title="skfolio.prior.FactorModel.cs_regression_t_stats"><code class="xref py py-attr docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">cs_regression_t_stats</span></code></a> returns the
per-observation t-statistic of each factor return, computed in the reduced basis
when constraints are active. Values of <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(|t| &gt; 2\)</span> indicate significance at
approximately the 5% level.
<a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.prior.FactorModel.html#skfolio.prior.FactorModel.plot_cs_regression_t_stats" title="skfolio.prior.FactorModel.plot_cs_regression_t_stats"><code class="xref py py-meth docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">plot_cs_regression_t_stats</span></code></a> displays them
through time.</p>
<p><a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.prior.FactorModel.html#skfolio.prior.FactorModel.cs_regression_t_stat_exceedance_rate" title="skfolio.prior.FactorModel.cs_regression_t_stat_exceedance_rate"><code class="xref py py-meth docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">cs_regression_t_stat_exceedance_rate</span></code></a>
aggregates this through time. A factor whose true coefficient is zero would
exceed the threshold about 5% of the time. Rates persistently above this
reference indicate a factor that is repeatedly significant in the cross-section.
<a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.prior.FactorModel.html#skfolio.prior.FactorModel.plot_cs_regression_t_stat_exceedance_rate" title="skfolio.prior.FactorModel.plot_cs_regression_t_stat_exceedance_rate"><code class="xref py py-meth docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">plot_cs_regression_t_stat_exceedance_rate</span></code></a>
displays the exceedance rates:</p>
<div class="highlight-python notranslate"><div class="highlight"><pre><span></span><span class="n">factor_model</span><span class="o">.</span><span class="n">plot_cs_regression_t_stat_exceedance_rate</span><span class="p">(</span><span class="n">families</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="p">[</span><span class="s2">"market"</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="s2">"style"</span><span class="p">])</span>
</pre></div>
</div>

       alt="Share of dates on which each market or style factor return has an absolute t-statistic above two"

</section>
<section id="risk-forecasting">
<span id="factor-model-risk-forecasting"></span><h2>Risk Forecasting<a class="headerlink" href="#risk-forecasting" title="Link to this heading">#</a></h2>
<p>The risk forecast combines the distribution of factor returns and the idiosyncratic risk of each asset.
This section details their estimation and how they are assembled into the asset covariance and
return scenarios.</p>
<section id="factor-return-distribution">
<span id="factor-model-factor-return-distribution"></span><h3>Factor Return Distribution<a class="headerlink" href="#factor-return-distribution" title="Link to this heading">#</a></h3>
<p>The estimated factor return time series is passed to <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">factor_prior_estimator</span></code>, a
<a class="reference internal" href="prior.html#prior"><span class="std std-ref">prior estimator</span></a> that produces the expected factor returns (factor
premia), the factor covariance and factor return scenarios. When zero-sum constraints
are present, the estimation runs in the reduced full-rank basis, making the factor
covariance positive definite by construction.</p>
<p>The default is <a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.prior.EmpiricalPrior.html#skfolio.prior.EmpiricalPrior" title="skfolio.prior.EmpiricalPrior"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">EmpiricalPrior</span></code></a> with
<a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.moments.EWMu.html#skfolio.moments.EWMu" title="skfolio.moments.EWMu"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">EWMu</span></code></a> for expected returns and
<a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.moments.RegimeAdjustedEWCovariance.html#skfolio.moments.RegimeAdjustedEWCovariance" title="skfolio.moments.RegimeAdjustedEWCovariance"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">RegimeAdjustedEWCovariance</span></code></a> for covariance. The latter
addresses two known weaknesses of plain exponentially weighted covariance:</p>
<ul class="simple">
<li><p>It applies a scalar regime multiplier (Short-Term Volatility Update) that improves
risk calibration when volatility regimes change faster than the EWMA half-life can
track.</p></li>
<li><p>It supports separate half-lives for variance and correlation. Empirically,
volatility mean-reverts faster than correlation: a shorter variance half-life adapts
quickly to volatility shifts while a longer correlation half-life reduces
estimation noise on co-movements.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>It also supports optional Newey-West HAC correction through <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">hac_lags</span></code> to adjust for
serial correlation in factor returns.</p>
<p>Because the factor return series is low-dimensional (<span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(K \ll N\)</span>), estimators
that would be unstable or expensive on thousands of assets are cheap and reliable on
factors. The factor prior is fully replaceable, e.g. covariance shrinkage or denoising can
be applied at the factor level, and views on factors can be expressed by using
<a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.prior.EntropyPooling.html#skfolio.prior.EntropyPooling" title="skfolio.prior.EntropyPooling"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">EntropyPooling</span></code></a> or <a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.prior.OpinionPooling.html#skfolio.prior.OpinionPooling" title="skfolio.prior.OpinionPooling"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">OpinionPooling</span></code></a> as
<code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">factor_prior_estimator</span></code>. The factor return scenarios produced here feed
scenario-based risk measures (e.g. CVaR) downstream.</p>
<p><a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.prior.FactorModel.html#skfolio.prior.FactorModel.plot_factor_forecast_correlation" title="skfolio.prior.FactorModel.plot_factor_forecast_correlation"><code class="xref py py-meth docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">plot_factor_forecast_correlation</span></code></a> shows the
factor return correlation forecast from the fitted factor covariance. High values
flag factors whose returns move together and carry overlapping risk. Industry
factors are omitted below for readability:</p>
<div class="highlight-python notranslate"><div class="highlight"><pre><span></span><span class="n">factor_model</span><span class="o">.</span><span class="n">plot_factor_forecast_correlation</span><span class="p">(</span><span class="n">families</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="p">[</span><span class="s2">"market"</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="s2">"style"</span><span class="p">])</span>
</pre></div>
</div>
<figure class="factor-model-figure">
  <img src="../_static/factor_model/plots/factor_model_factor_forecast_correlation.webp"
       alt="Forecast return correlation heatmap for market and style factors"
       width="1100" height="616"
       loading="eager" decoding="async"
       style="display:block;width:100%;height:auto;">
</figure><p>In the <a class="reference internal" href="#factor-model-code-example"><span class="std std-ref">example</span></a>, most correlations are
moderate, indicating that the factors capture distinct risk dimensions. The 0.80 market-beta
correlation is the known exception. The beta factor return is the
reward for holding high-beta names over low-beta names, a spread that widens in
rising markets and reverses in falling markets, making it co-move with the market return
by construction. The two factors remain separated in the regression because their
exposures are near-orthogonal in the cross-section, and the co-movement of their
returns is captured by the factor covariance.</p>
<p><a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.prior.FactorModel.html#skfolio.prior.FactorModel.plot_factor_forecast_volatilities" title="skfolio.prior.FactorModel.plot_factor_forecast_volatilities"><code class="xref py py-meth docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">plot_factor_forecast_volatilities</span></code></a> shows each
factor’s annualized volatility forecast which is calculated as the square root
of the factor covariance diagonal. It ranks the factors by their standalone risk
contribution:</p>
<div class="highlight-python notranslate"><div class="highlight"><pre><span></span><span class="n">factor_model</span><span class="o">.</span><span class="n">plot_factor_forecast_volatilities</span><span class="p">(</span><span class="n">families</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="p">[</span><span class="s2">"market"</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="s2">"style"</span><span class="p">])</span>
</pre></div>
</div>
<figure class="factor-model-figure">
  <img src="../_static/factor_model/plots/factor_model_factor_forecast_volatilities.webp"
       alt="Annualized forecast volatilities for market and style factors"
       width="1100" height="616"
       loading="lazy" decoding="async"
       style="display:block;width:100%;height:auto;">
</figure><p>The market factor dominates at close to 20% annualized volatility. Its return is
the benchmark return, while style factor returns are long-short spreads across
standardized exposures. The beta, momentum, size and liquidity factors form the
next tier around 5% to 8%, and the remaining styles sit near 2%.</p>
</section>
<section id="idiosyncratic-risk">
<h3>Idiosyncratic Risk<a class="headerlink" href="#idiosyncratic-risk" title="Link to this heading">#</a></h3>
<p>Per-asset idiosyncratic variances are estimated from the idiosyncratic returns by
<code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">idio_variance_estimator</span></code>, defaulting to
<a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.moments.RegimeAdjustedEWVariance.html#skfolio.moments.RegimeAdjustedEWVariance" title="skfolio.moments.RegimeAdjustedEWVariance"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">RegimeAdjustedEWVariance</span></code></a>. The estimator must support
<code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">partial_fit</span></code> so the model can recover per-asset variance estimates at each
observation, stored in <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">factor_model_.idio_variances</span></code>. Assets still in their
<a class="reference internal" href="#factor-model-warmup"><span class="std std-ref">warmup period</span></a> have NaN variances, which propagate
to the fitted moments and mark them as not yet investable.</p>
<p>A factor model assumes the factor structure captures all common risk, leaving
idiosyncratic returns uncorrelated across assets. The idiosyncratic covariance is
therefore diagonal by default. In practice, linked securities remain correlated
after the factor structure is removed (e.g. multiple share classes, ADRs versus
ordinary shares, or dual listings). Without correction, optimizers treat such
pairs as diversified sources of idiosyncratic risk and may over-allocate to them.</p>
<p>Correlation thresholding addresses these cases. When <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">idio_corr_threshold</span></code> is set to
<span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(\tau &gt; 0\)</span>, the <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">idio_corr_estimator</span></code> (defaulting to
<a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.moments.EWCovariance.html#skfolio.moments.EWCovariance" title="skfolio.moments.EWCovariance"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">EWCovariance</span></code></a>) is fitted on idiosyncratic returns
standardized by their contemporaneous idiosyncratic volatility. Only the correlation
component of its output is retained: off-diagonal correlations with
<span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(|\rho_{ij}| \le \tau\)</span> are set to zero, and the surviving correlations are
recombined with the latest per-asset variances to form a sparse idiosyncratic
covariance. Variances and correlations are estimated separately because a single full
covariance estimator would mix per-asset variance estimation with off-diagonal
correlation noise. This keeps variances driven by <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">idio_variance_estimator</span></code> and
applies the correlation overlay only where residual correlations are large enough to
retain.</p>
<p>Persistent residual correlation across a broader group, such as sub-industry
peers under a coarse industry classification, signals a missing factor rather
than a thresholding problem. Adding the factor keeps the idiosyncratic covariance
sparse, while lowering <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(\tau\)</span> to absorb the group reintroduces the
estimation noise the diagonal assumption avoids.</p>
</section>
<section id="idiosyncratic-risk-calibration">
<h3>Idiosyncratic Risk Calibration<a class="headerlink" href="#idiosyncratic-risk-calibration" title="Link to this heading">#</a></h3>
<p>These diagnostics test the idiosyncratic volatility forecasts through the
standardized idiosyncratic returns
<span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(z_{it} = \epsilon_{it} / \hat\sigma_{it}\)</span>. Under correct calibration,
<span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(z\)</span> has cross-sectional standard deviation 1.0.</p>
<p><a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.prior.FactorModel.html#skfolio.prior.FactorModel.idio_calibration_summary" title="skfolio.prior.FactorModel.idio_calibration_summary"><code class="xref py py-meth docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">idio_calibration_summary</span></code></a> aggregates the main
statistics:</p>
<ul class="simple">
<li><p><code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">mean_cs_std</span></code> close to 1.0 indicates correctly scaled idiosyncratic risk. Values
persistently above 1.0 indicate underestimated risk and persistently below 1.0
indicate overestimated risk.</p></li>
<li><p><code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">mean_tail_rate_3sigma</span></code> is the fraction of standardized returns beyond three
standard deviations. The Gaussian reference is 0.27%, and values of 1% to 3% are
common for equity factor models due to fat tails.</p></li>
<li><p><code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">mean_cs_excess_kurtosis</span></code> above zero and a moderate <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">mean_cs_skewness</span></code> are typical.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>Two complementary statistics separate ranking power from calibration.
<a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.prior.FactorModel.html#skfolio.prior.FactorModel.idio_vol_ic" title="skfolio.prior.FactorModel.idio_vol_ic"><code class="xref py py-attr docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">idio_vol_ic</span></code></a> is the Spearman correlation between
predicted volatility and the next-period absolute idiosyncratic return with high
values meaning that the model ranks cross-sectional volatility differences well.
<a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.prior.FactorModel.html#skfolio.prior.FactorModel.idio_vol_residual_dependence" title="skfolio.prior.FactorModel.idio_vol_residual_dependence"><code class="xref py py-attr docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">idio_vol_residual_dependence</span></code></a> is the same
correlation after standardizing the next-period return by the predicted
volatility: under correct calibration it should be close to 0. The desirable
pattern is a high <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">idio_vol_ic</span></code> combined with residual dependence near 0.</p>
<p><a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.prior.FactorModel.html#skfolio.prior.FactorModel.plot_idio_calibration" title="skfolio.prior.FactorModel.plot_idio_calibration"><code class="xref py py-meth docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">plot_idio_calibration</span></code></a> tracks the
cross-sectional standard deviation of the standardized returns through time:</p>
<div class="highlight-python notranslate"><div class="highlight"><pre><span></span><span class="n">factor_model</span><span class="o">.</span><span class="n">plot_idio_calibration</span><span class="p">(</span><span class="n">window</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="mi">20</span><span class="p">)</span>
</pre></div>
</div>
<figure class="factor-model-figure">
  <img src="../_static/factor_model/plots/factor_model_idio_calibration.webp"
       alt="Rolling cross-sectional standard deviation of standardized idiosyncratic returns relative to the calibration target"
       width="1100" height="616"
       loading="lazy" decoding="async"
       style="display:block;width:100%;height:auto;">
</figure><p>In the <a class="reference internal" href="#factor-model-code-example"><span class="std std-ref">example</span></a>, the series oscillates around a
mean of 1.06, a slight average underestimation of idiosyncratic risk. The spike
above 1.5 at the COVID shock shows realized dispersion outrunning the forecasts,
followed by a dip below 0.7 as the variance estimator caught up while volatility
dissipated.</p>
<p><a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.prior.FactorModel.html#skfolio.prior.FactorModel.plot_idio_vol_ic" title="skfolio.prior.FactorModel.plot_idio_vol_ic"><code class="xref py py-meth docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">plot_idio_vol_ic</span></code></a> displays the volatility rank
IC through time:</p>
<div class="highlight-python notranslate"><div class="highlight"><pre><span></span><span class="n">factor_model</span><span class="o">.</span><span class="n">plot_idio_vol_ic</span><span class="p">()</span>
</pre></div>
</div>
<figure class="factor-model-figure">
  <img src="../_static/factor_model/plots/factor_model_idio_vol_ic.webp"
       alt="Information coefficient through time for predicted versus realized idiosyncratic volatility ranks"
       width="1100" height="616"
       loading="lazy" decoding="async"
       style="display:block;width:100%;height:auto;">
</figure><p>The rolling mean holds near 0.4 across the sample: the model consistently
forecasts higher volatility for assets that subsequently realize larger
idiosyncratic moves. Together with the calibration series close to 1.0, the
idiosyncratic risk forecasts are both well ordered and well scaled.</p>
<p><a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.prior.FactorModel.html#skfolio.prior.FactorModel" title="skfolio.prior.FactorModel"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">FactorModel</span></code></a> provides additional idiosyncratic risk
diagnostics:</p>
<ul class="simple">
<li><p><a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.prior.FactorModel.html#skfolio.prior.FactorModel.plot_idio_tail_rate" title="skfolio.prior.FactorModel.plot_idio_tail_rate"><code class="xref py py-meth docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">plot_idio_tail_rate</span></code></a></p></li>
<li><p><a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.prior.FactorModel.html#skfolio.prior.FactorModel.plot_idio_kurtosis" title="skfolio.prior.FactorModel.plot_idio_kurtosis"><code class="xref py py-meth docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">plot_idio_kurtosis</span></code></a></p></li>
<li><p><a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.prior.FactorModel.html#skfolio.prior.FactorModel.plot_idio_skewness" title="skfolio.prior.FactorModel.plot_idio_skewness"><code class="xref py py-meth docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">plot_idio_skewness</span></code></a></p></li>
<li><p><a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.prior.FactorModel.html#skfolio.prior.FactorModel.plot_idio_vol_residual_dependence" title="skfolio.prior.FactorModel.plot_idio_vol_residual_dependence"><code class="xref py py-meth docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">plot_idio_vol_residual_dependence</span></code></a></p></li>
</ul>
</section>
<section id="asset-covariance-forecast">
<span id="factor-model-asset-covariance-forecast"></span><h3>Asset Covariance Forecast<a class="headerlink" href="#asset-covariance-forecast" title="Link to this heading">#</a></h3>
<p>The asset covariance forecast assembles the pieces estimated above:</p>
<div class="math notranslate nohighlight">
\[\Sigma = B(T)\,F\,B(T)^\top + D\]</div>
<p>where <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(B(T)\)</span> is the latest loading matrix, <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(F\)</span> the factor covariance
and <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(D\)</span> the idiosyncratic covariance. The result is positive definite and
well conditioned by construction as the systematic part is low-rank positive
semidefinite and <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(D\)</span> is positive diagonal (or sparse positive definite).</p>
<p>Asset return scenarios follow the same construction. Factor scenarios from the
factor prior are mapped through the latest loading matrix, and idiosyncratic
scenarios calibrated to the latest idiosyncratic risk forecast are added on top.
Optimizers using scenario-based risk measures (e.g. CVaR) therefore see both
return components.</p>
<p>skfolio exploits this structure when passing the asset covariance to downstream
optimizers. Portfolio variance splits into a factor contribution and an
idiosyncratic contribution:</p>
<div class="math notranslate nohighlight">
\[w^\top \Sigma\, w
= \lVert F^{1/2} B^\top w \rVert^2 + \lVert D^{1/2} w \rVert^2\]</div>
<p>Both terms involve only small matrices: the <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(n \times K\)</span> loading matrix,
the <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(K \times K\)</span> factor covariance and the <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(n\)</span> idiosyncratic
variances. skfolio’s convex optimizers build their risk constraints on these
directly, through <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">factor_model_.covariance_sqrt</span></code>, instead of assembling and
factorizing the dense <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(n \times n\)</span> covariance. On a universe of thousands
of assets driven by a few dozen factors, this keeps the risk constraints small
and the optimization fast.</p>
</section>
<section id="covariance-forecast-evaluation">
<span id="factor-model-covariance-forecast-evaluation"></span><h3>Covariance Forecast Evaluation<a class="headerlink" href="#covariance-forecast-evaluation" title="Link to this heading">#</a></h3>
<p>In-sample fit does not measure forecast accuracy. The covariance forecast is
evaluated out of sample with
<a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.model_selection.covariance_forecast_evaluation.html#skfolio.model_selection.covariance_forecast_evaluation" title="skfolio.model_selection.covariance_forecast_evaluation"><code class="xref py py-func docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">covariance_forecast_evaluation</span></code></a> or, using online
learning for speed, <a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.model_selection.online_covariance_forecast_evaluation.html#skfolio.model_selection.online_covariance_forecast_evaluation" title="skfolio.model_selection.online_covariance_forecast_evaluation"><code class="xref py py-func docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">online_covariance_forecast_evaluation</span></code></a>.
Both walk forward through the data, compare each covariance forecast with the
subsequently realized returns over an evaluation window and return a
<a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.model_selection.CovarianceForecastEvaluation.html#skfolio.model_selection.CovarianceForecastEvaluation" title="skfolio.model_selection.CovarianceForecastEvaluation"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">CovarianceForecastEvaluation</span></code></a> with summary
statistics and plots. Four diagnostics are computed:</p>
<ul class="simple">
<li><p>The <strong>Mahalanobis calibration ratio</strong> tests the full covariance structure across
all eigenvalue directions. The target is 1.0, with values above 1.0 indicating
underestimated risk and below 1.0 indicating overestimated risk.</p></li>
<li><p>The <strong>diagonal calibration ratio</strong> applies the same test to individual asset
variances, ignoring correlations.</p></li>
<li><p>The <strong>portfolio bias statistic</strong> tests covariance calibration along
user-supplied portfolio directions. For each test portfolio, realized
portfolio returns are divided by their forecast volatility, and the standard
deviation of that standardized series should be 1.0 under correct
calibration. Evaluating several representative portfolios can reveal
direction-specific under or over-estimated risk.</p></li>
<li><p>The <strong>portfolio QLIKE</strong> scores portfolio variance forecasts, with lower values
indicating better forecasts.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>The figures below evaluate the model with
<a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.model_selection.online_covariance_forecast_evaluation.html#skfolio.model_selection.online_covariance_forecast_evaluation" title="skfolio.model_selection.online_covariance_forecast_evaluation"><code class="xref py py-func docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">online_covariance_forecast_evaluation</span></code></a> and
<code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">test_size=5</span></code>:</p>
<div class="highlight-python notranslate"><div class="highlight"><pre><span></span><span class="kn">from</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="nn">skfolio.model_selection</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="kn">import</span> <span class="n">online_covariance_forecast_evaluation</span>

<span class="n">evaluation</span> <span class="o">=</span> <span class="n">online_covariance_forecast_evaluation</span><span class="p">(</span>
    <span class="n">model</span><span class="p">,</span>
    <span class="n">X</span><span class="p">,</span>
    <span class="n">params</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="p">{</span><span class="s2">"characteristics"</span><span class="p">:</span> <span class="n">characteristics</span><span class="p">},</span>
    <span class="n">warmup_size</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="mi">2</span> <span class="o">*</span> <span class="mi">252</span> <span class="o">+</span> <span class="mi">21</span><span class="p">,</span>
    <span class="n">test_size</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="mi">5</span><span class="p">,</span>
<span class="p">)</span>
<span class="n">evaluation</span><span class="o">.</span><span class="n">plot_calibration</span><span class="p">()</span>
</pre></div>
</div>
<figure class="factor-model-figure">
  <img src="../_static/factor_model/plots/covariance_eval_calibration.webp"
       alt="Rolling Mahalanobis, diagonal and portfolio bias calibration ratios for the covariance forecast"
       width="1100" height="616"
       loading="lazy" decoding="async"
       style="display:block;width:100%;height:auto;">
</figure><p>In the <a class="reference internal" href="#factor-model-code-example"><span class="std std-ref">example</span></a>, the diagonal ratio and the bias
statistic oscillate around the target, implying individual asset variances and
test-portfolio volatilities are well scaled. The Mahalanobis ratio stays near 1.5,
indicating that the remaining underestimation is concentrated in the covariance’s
low-variance directions. The Mahalanobis distance gives more weight to errors in
those directions, while the diagonal ratio and portfolio bias statistic are less
sensitive to them. All three ratios spike at the 2020 shock. The diagonal ratio and
bias statistic then fall below 1.0 through 2021 as the forecasts lag the post-crisis
decline in volatility, while the Mahalanobis ratio falls back toward its target
without crossing it. This low-variance underestimation originates mostly in the
idiosyncratic block (residual correlations and missing factors) and is addressed
with <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">idio_corr_threshold</span></code> and the optimizer-level regularization covered in
<a class="reference internal" href="#factor-model-orthogonal-space-regularization"><span class="std std-ref">Orthogonal Space Regularization</span></a>.</p>
<p>The summary table aggregates the four diagnostics over the full evaluation
period:</p>
<div class="highlight-python notranslate"><div class="highlight"><pre><span></span><span class="n">evaluation</span><span class="o">.</span><span class="n">summary</span><span class="p">()</span>
</pre></div>

additional plots:</p>
<ul class="simple">
<li><p><a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.model_selection.CovarianceForecastEvaluation.html#skfolio.model_selection.CovarianceForecastEvaluation.plot_qlike_loss" title="skfolio.model_selection.CovarianceForecastEvaluation.plot_qlike_loss"><code class="xref py py-meth docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">plot_qlike_loss</span></code></a></p></li>
<li><p><a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.model_selection.CovarianceForecastEvaluation.html#skfolio.model_selection.CovarianceForecastEvaluation.plot_exceedance" title="skfolio.model_selection.CovarianceForecastEvaluation.plot_exceedance"><code class="xref py py-meth docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">plot_exceedance</span></code></a></p></li>
</ul>
<p><a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.model_selection.CovarianceForecastComparison.html#skfolio.model_selection.CovarianceForecastComparison" title="skfolio.model_selection.CovarianceForecastComparison"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">CovarianceForecastComparison</span></code></a> runs the same
evaluation over several models and aggregates the results for side-by-side
comparison. Missing data follows the panel conventions: only finite observations
contribute and inactive assets are excluded.</p>
<p>The comparison below contrasts two
<a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.moments.RegimeAdjustedEWCovariance.html#skfolio.moments.RegimeAdjustedEWCovariance" title="skfolio.moments.RegimeAdjustedEWCovariance"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">RegimeAdjustedEWCovariance</span></code></a> regime half-lives for the
factor prior: <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">regime_half_life=month</span></code> (21 trading days) against
<code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">regime_half_life=quarter</span></code> (63 trading days), which reacts more slowly to
volatility regime shifts.</p>
<div class="highlight-python notranslate"><div class="highlight"><pre><span></span><span class="kn">from</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="nn">skfolio.model_selection</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="kn">import</span> <span class="n">CovarianceForecastComparison</span>

<span class="p">)</span>
