</div><p><a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.model_selection.CovarianceForecastComparison.html#skfolio.model_selection.CovarianceForecastComparison" title="skfolio.model_selection.CovarianceForecastComparison"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">CovarianceForecastComparison</span></code></a> provides
additional plots:</p>
<ul class="simple">
<li><p><a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.model_selection.CovarianceForecastComparison.html#skfolio.model_selection.CovarianceForecastComparison.plot_calibration" title="skfolio.model_selection.CovarianceForecastComparison.plot_calibration"><code class="xref py py-meth docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">plot_calibration</span></code></a>
with full diagnostics (Mahalanobis, diagonal, bias)</p></li>
<li><p><a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.model_selection.CovarianceForecastComparison.html#skfolio.model_selection.CovarianceForecastComparison.plot_exceedance" title="skfolio.model_selection.CovarianceForecastComparison.plot_exceedance"><code class="xref py py-meth docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">plot_exceedance</span></code></a></p></li>
</ul>
</section>
</section>
<section id="expected-returns">
<span id="factor-model-expected-returns"></span><h2>Expected Returns<a class="headerlink" href="#expected-returns" title="Link to this heading">#</a></h2>
<p><a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.prior.CharacteristicsFactorModel.html#skfolio.prior.CharacteristicsFactorModel" title="skfolio.prior.CharacteristicsFactorModel"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">CharacteristicsFactorModel</span></code></a> estimates expected asset returns
from factor premia and an optional alpha forecast. The factor prior estimates expected
factor returns, which are mapped to assets through their exposures. This section
explains how these estimates are used together and how they are evaluated.</p>
<section id="expected-factor-returns">
<h3>Expected Factor Returns<a class="headerlink" href="#expected-factor-returns" title="Link to this heading">#</a></h3>
<p>With <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">alpha_estimator=None</span></code> and the default <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">spanned_alpha_shrinkage=1</span></code>, expected
asset returns are determined entirely by the factor premia:</p>
<div class="math notranslate nohighlight">
\[\mu = B(T)\,\mu_f\]</div>
<p>where <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(\mu_f\)</span> holds the expected factor returns estimated by
<code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">factor_prior_estimator</span></code> (see <a class="reference internal" href="#factor-model-factor-return-distribution"><span class="std std-ref">Factor Return Distribution</span></a>) and <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(B(T)\)</span> is the latest
loading matrix. Each asset’s expected return is the sum of the premia of the
factors it is exposed to, weighted by its exposures.</p>
</section>
<section id="information-coefficient">
<h3>Information Coefficient<a class="headerlink" href="#information-coefficient" title="Link to this heading">#</a></h3>
<p><a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.prior.FactorModel.html#skfolio.prior.FactorModel.exposure_ic_summary" title="skfolio.prior.FactorModel.exposure_ic_summary"><code class="xref py py-meth docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">exposure_ic_summary</span></code></a> measures the
cross-sectional correlation between factor exposures at <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(t\)</span> and the forward
mean asset return from <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(t+1\)</span> to <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(t+h\)</span>, where <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(h\)</span> is the
<code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">horizon</span></code> parameter. The default <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">correlation_method</span></code> computes the Spearman rank
IC, with Pearson IC weighted by the regression weights as the alternative. The
summary reports <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">mean_ic</span></code>, <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">std_ic</span></code>, <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">ic_ir</span></code> (mean over standard deviation) and
<code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">hit_rate</span></code> per factor.
<a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.prior.FactorModel.html#skfolio.prior.FactorModel.plot_cumulative_exposure_ic" title="skfolio.prior.FactorModel.plot_cumulative_exposure_ic"><code class="xref py py-meth docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">plot_cumulative_exposure_ic</span></code></a> shows the
cumulative IC through time:</p>
<div class="highlight-python notranslate"><div class="highlight"><pre><span></span><span class="n">factor_model</span><span class="o">.</span><span class="n">plot_cumulative_exposure_ic</span><span class="p">(</span><span class="n">families</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="p">[</span><span class="s2">"market"</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="s2">"style"</span><span class="p">])</span>
</pre></div>
</div>
<figure class="factor-model-figure">

       alt="Cumulative information coefficients between factor exposures and next-period asset returns"

</div><p>Daily ICs are small in absolute value, and the persistence of their sign matters
more than their level. In the <a class="reference internal" href="#factor-model-code-example"><span class="std std-ref">example</span></a>,
momentum and profitability accumulate positive IC steadily across the sample
(hit rates of 56% and 55%), while volatility, liquidity and value accumulate
negative IC.</p>
<p>The IC quantifies return-predictive power. In a risk model, factors are designed
to forecast covariance, not expected returns. A factor can
therefore be an excellent risk factor with an IC near zero, and a low IC is not
a reason to discard it. IC is mainly useful for evaluating alpha signals and
factor premia, not for deciding whether a factor should remain in a risk model.</p>
</section>
<section id="alpha-estimators">
<span id="factor-model-alpha"></span><h3>Alpha Estimators<a class="headerlink" href="#alpha-estimators" title="Link to this heading">#</a></h3>
<p>The <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">alpha_estimator</span></code> parameter accepts any <a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.alpha.BaseAlpha.html#skfolio.alpha.BaseAlpha" title="skfolio.alpha.BaseAlpha"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">BaseAlpha</span></code></a>
estimator producing an expected-return forecast for each asset. Before it is fitted,
the factor model enriches the panel with the quantities it has already estimated:
idiosyncratic returns, idiosyncratic variances, regression weights, benchmark
weights and the factor exposure tensor. In typical alpha research workflows,
idiosyncratic returns serve as the prediction target (typically after
cross-sectional transformation), idiosyncratic variances scale the target and
factor exposures neutralize the features. Targeting idiosyncratic returns rather
than raw returns removes the factor-driven component from the target. The
cross-sectional variation of raw returns includes each asset’s factor exposures
multiplied by the factor returns, so a signal correlated with the exposures would
pick up factor premia already captured by the factor model. The idiosyncratic
target also carries less noise, since common factor volatility is removed.</p>
<p>The alpha forecast should be expressed in expected-return units when it is combined
with expected factor returns or used in an optimization alongside return-denominated
quantities (e.g. transaction costs, turnover constraints, return targets). Unitless
cross-sectional scores are appropriate only when the downstream objective treats
them as ordinal signals.</p>
<p>skfolio provides alpha estimators following the same signal pipeline as
factor exposures with descriptors being transformed into cross-sectional scores
(<code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">outlier_transformer</span></code>, <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">scoring_transformer</span></code>, <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">transform_by_group</span></code>), optionally
neutralized against factor exposures (<code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">neutralize_against</span></code>) and re-scored. The
estimators differ mainly in how the scored descriptors are combined and how the
result is scaled into expected-return units.</p>
<ul class="simple">
<li><p><a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.alpha.FixedWeightedAlpha.html#skfolio.alpha.FixedWeightedAlpha" title="skfolio.alpha.FixedWeightedAlpha"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">FixedWeightedAlpha</span></code></a> combines multiple descriptors
with fixed signed weights and a fixed <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">forecast_scale</span></code>. The weights define the
direction and relative contribution of each descriptor, while <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">forecast_scale</span></code>
converts one unit of composite score into the selected <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">forecast_unit</span></code>. This is useful
when the signal combination is specified outside the model and the main decision is
how strongly it should affect expected returns. <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">forecast_scale</span></code> requires careful
calibration as a value too large leads the optimizer to over-allocate to the alpha
forecast, whereas a value which is too small leaves the signal with little effect after
costs, risk limits and turnover constraints.</p></li>
<li><p><a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.alpha.EWSharpeOptimalAlpha.html#skfolio.alpha.EWSharpeOptimalAlpha" title="skfolio.alpha.EWSharpeOptimalAlpha"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">EWSharpeOptimalAlpha</span></code></a> combines descriptors linearly and
estimates their coefficients with exponentially weighted least squares on forward
idiosyncratic returns, using inverse idiosyncratic variance as regression weights.
It learns both sign and scale from realized data and accounts for cross-descriptor
correlations, scaling the signal blend according to its estimated idiosyncratic
return payoff. <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">forecast_scale</span></code> is then applied to the learned return-unit
forecast as a final strength multiplier. The learned coefficients are subject to
sampling error: short half-lives, noisy targets, weak descriptors and descriptors
whose predictive content is already absorbed by the factor model produce unstable
coefficients that can degrade an otherwise useful raw signal.</p></li>
<li><p><a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.alpha.PredictorAlpha.html#skfolio.alpha.PredictorAlpha" title="skfolio.alpha.PredictorAlpha"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">PredictorAlpha</span></code></a> wraps a user-provided
scikit-learn regressor and treats each observation-asset pair as one training sample.
It supports nonlinear interactions and non-additive signal effects, for example with
tree-based models or regularized regressors. With <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">calibrate_to_return_units=True</span></code>,
the raw predictor output is calibrated to expected-return units by exponentially
weighted least squares, so <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">alpha_</span></code> is in expected-return units.
<code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">forecast_scale</span></code> is applied after this optional calibration. This flexibility
increases the need for robust validation, because nonlinear models can fit noise,
require more data and be sensitive to target construction and cross-validation
design.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>All three estimators produce <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">alpha_</span></code> in expected-return units. The
<code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">forecast_unit</span></code> parameter controls the unit of the intermediate forecast
(<a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.alpha.ForecastUnit.html#skfolio.alpha.ForecastUnit" title="skfolio.alpha.ForecastUnit"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">ForecastUnit</span></code></a>). With <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">ForecastUnit.IDIO_RETURN</span></code> (default),
the forecast is interpreted directly as expected idiosyncratic return. With
<code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">ForecastUnit.IDIO_SHARPE</span></code>, the forecast is interpreted as idiosyncratic Sharpe and
multiplied by the current idiosyncratic volatility before being passed to the
factor model. The Sharpe unit is preferable when a signal is expected to rank
risk-adjusted opportunities rather than raw returns. <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">forecast_scale</span></code> is the
common final multiplier controlling alpha strength.</p>
<p>The following example combines two reversal descriptors, <a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.descriptor.ReturnOnAssets.html#skfolio.descriptor.ReturnOnAssets" title="skfolio.descriptor.ReturnOnAssets"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">return</span> <span class="pre">on</span> <span class="pre">assets</span></code></a>,
and <a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.descriptor.EWAmihudIlliquidity.html#skfolio.descriptor.EWAmihudIlliquidity" title="skfolio.descriptor.EWAmihudIlliquidity"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">Amihud</span> <span class="pre">illiquidity</span></code></a> with fixed signed weights and uses a Gaussian rank scorer
for the cross-sectional scoring:</p>
<div class="highlight-python notranslate"><div class="highlight"><pre><span></span><span class="kn">from</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="nn">skfolio.alpha</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="kn">import</span> <span class="n">FixedWeightedAlpha</span>
<span class="kn">from</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="nn">skfolio.descriptor</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="kn">import</span> <span class="n">EWAmihudIlliquidity</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="n">ReturnOnAssets</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="n">Reversal</span>
<span class="kn">from</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="nn">skfolio.preprocessing</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="kn">import</span> <span class="n">CSGaussianRankScaler</span>

<span class="n">alpha_estimator</span> <span class="o">=</span> <span class="n">FixedWeightedAlpha</span><span class="p">(</span>
    <span class="n">descriptors</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="p">[</span>
        <span class="p">(</span><span class="s2">"reversal_5d"</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="n">Reversal</span><span class="p">(</span><span class="n">window</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="mi">5</span><span class="p">)),</span>
        <span class="p">(</span><span class="s2">"reversal_21d"</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="n">Reversal</span><span class="p">(</span><span class="n">window</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="mi">21</span><span class="p">)),</span>
        <span class="p">(</span><span class="s2">"return_on_assets"</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="n">ReturnOnAssets</span><span class="p">()),</span>
        <span class="p">(</span><span class="s2">"amihud_illiquidity"</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="n">EWAmihudIlliquidity</span><span class="p">()),</span>
    <span class="p">],</span>
    <span class="n">weights</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="p">[</span><span class="mf">1.0</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="mf">1.0</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="o">-</span><span class="mf">1.0</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="o">-</span><span class="mf">1.0</span><span class="p">],</span>
    <span class="n">forecast_scale</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="mf">0.0001</span><span class="p">,</span>
    <span class="n">scoring_transformer</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="n">CSGaussianRankScaler</span><span class="p">(),</span>
    <span class="n">n_jobs</span><span class="o">=-</span><span class="mi">1</span><span class="p">,</span>
<span class="p">)</span>
</pre></div>
</div>
<p>In practice, the simplest estimator that matches the research assumption is usually
the most robust starting point. Use <a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.alpha.FixedWeightedAlpha.html#skfolio.alpha.FixedWeightedAlpha" title="skfolio.alpha.FixedWeightedAlpha"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">FixedWeightedAlpha</span></code></a>
when signal direction and relative weights are already specified and the main
decision is scale. Use <a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.alpha.EWSharpeOptimalAlpha.html#skfolio.alpha.EWSharpeOptimalAlpha" title="skfolio.alpha.EWSharpeOptimalAlpha"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">EWSharpeOptimalAlpha</span></code></a> when
the signal combination is expected to be approximately linear and there is enough
history to estimate stable payoffs. Use <a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.alpha.PredictorAlpha.html#skfolio.alpha.PredictorAlpha" title="skfolio.alpha.PredictorAlpha"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">PredictorAlpha</span></code></a>
when nonlinear effects are important and the additional validation burden is
acceptable. Custom estimators are created by subclassing
<a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.alpha.BaseAlpha.html#skfolio.alpha.BaseAlpha" title="skfolio.alpha.BaseAlpha"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">BaseAlpha</span></code></a>. During the alpha estimator’s
<a class="reference internal" href="#factor-model-warmup"><span class="std std-ref">warmup period</span></a>, its forecast is treated as zero.</p>
</section>
<section id="spanned-and-orthogonal-alpha">
<span id="factor-model-spanned-alpha"></span><h3>Spanned and Orthogonal Alpha<a class="headerlink" href="#spanned-and-orthogonal-alpha" title="Link to this heading">#</a></h3>
<p>The fitted alpha forecast is decomposed into spanned alpha and orthogonal alpha by
projecting it onto the factor exposure space with a weighted cross-sectional
regression, using the latest exposures and regression weights:</p>
<div class="math notranslate nohighlight">
\[\alpha = \alpha^{\parallel} + \alpha^{\perp}
\qquad \text{with} \qquad
\alpha^{\parallel} = B(T)\,g\]</div>
<p>where <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(\alpha^{\parallel}\)</span> is the spanned alpha,
<span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(\alpha^{\perp}\)</span> is the orthogonal alpha and <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(g\)</span> is the factor-return
vector that reproduces the spanned alpha through <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(B(T)\)</span>.</p>
<p><code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">spanned_alpha_shrinkage</span></code> blends the factor-implied asset expected returns
<span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(B(T)\,\mu_f\)</span> with the spanned alpha:</p>
<div class="math notranslate nohighlight">
\[\mu^{\parallel}
= \lambda\,B(T)\,\mu_f + (1 - \lambda)\,\alpha^{\parallel}\]</div>
<p>where <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(\mu_f\)</span> contains expected factor returns estimated from the factor
return time series (see <a class="reference internal" href="#factor-model-factor-return-distribution"><span class="std std-ref">Factor Return Distribution</span></a>). <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(\lambda = 1\)</span> (default) uses
factor-implied asset expected returns, <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(\lambda = 0\)</span> uses the spanned alpha and
intermediate values blend the two. With the default, the alpha forecast contributes
to expected returns only through the orthogonal alpha.</p>
<p>The orthogonal alpha is shrunk towards zero by <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">orthogonal_alpha_confidence</span></code>:</p>
<div class="math notranslate nohighlight">
\[\mu = \mu^{\parallel} + c\,\alpha^{\perp}\]</div>
<p>where <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(c = 1\)</span> (default) uses the orthogonal alpha as-is and <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(c = 0\)</span>
discards it. Orthogonal directions are penalized only through idiosyncratic
variances in the covariance forecast, so an optimizer allocates to them
aggressively when they carry alpha. Reducing <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">orthogonal_alpha_confidence</span></code>
tempers this incentive when confidence in the forecast is limited.
<a class="reference internal" href="#factor-model-orthogonal-space-regularization"><span class="std std-ref">Orthogonal Space Regularization</span></a> covers this behavior and the
optimizer-level alternatives.</p>
<p>The shrunk orthogonal alpha <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(c\,\alpha^{\perp}\)</span> is stored in
<code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">factor_model_.idio_mu</span></code>. When currency factors are present, direct currency expected
returns are added to <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(\mu\)</span>.</p>
<p>With the default weighted least-squares cross-sectional regressor, the decomposition
satisfies <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(B(T)^\top W\alpha^{\perp}=0\)</span>. Custom robust or regularized
cross-sectional regressors may produce a component that is only approximately
orthogonal.</p>
</section>
<section id="alpha-and-risk-factor-alignment">
<h3>Alpha and Risk Factor Alignment<a class="headerlink" href="#alpha-and-risk-factor-alignment" title="Link to this heading">#</a></h3>
<p>A signal may closely resemble an existing risk-model factor while using a modified
definition that produces a stronger expected return. The key question is whether
the modified definition also provides a better representation of systematic risk.</p>
<p>If it explains common return variation better or improves risk forecasts, it should
replace the existing factor definition. Otherwise, the additional systematic
component would remain in idiosyncratic returns, causing the optimizer to underestimate
its risk.</p>
<p>If the modified definition improves expected returns but does not improve the risk
model, the existing risk factor should remain. The resulting alpha forecast can then
be decomposed into spanned alpha and orthogonal alpha. The orthogonal alpha is
diversifiable relative to the validated risk model, so allocating to it is intentional.</p>
<p>A historical example comes from momentum. Earlier commercial risk models measured
momentum over the most recent 12 months, including the latest month. Later
research separated medium-term momentum from short-term reversal by excluding
that latest month. A manager using the revised definition while retaining the older risk
model created a mismatch: the difference between the two definitions appeared
outside the modelled momentum factor and was treated as idiosyncratic risk. The optimizer
could therefore take a large position in that difference without accounting for its
systematic risk.</p>
<p>With skfolio, the definition can be tested directly and, when it provides a better
representation of systematic risk, used immediately in the risk model.</p>
</section>
<section id="orthogonal-space-regularization">
<span id="factor-model-orthogonal-space-regularization"></span><h3>Orthogonal Space Regularization<a class="headerlink" href="#orthogonal-space-regularization" title="Link to this heading">#</a></h3>
<p>The factor structure has an asymmetric effect on the covariance forecast. Systematic
directions carry the full factor covariance, while directions orthogonal to the
factor span are penalized only through the per-asset idiosyncratic variances, since
the idiosyncratic covariance is diagonal or sparse. To an optimizer, orthogonal
directions therefore appear cheap in risk, and any orthogonal alpha makes them
attractive, leading to concentrated allocations in the orthogonal space.</p>
<p>If the model were correctly specified, complete and free of estimation error, this
behavior would be desirable as factor-neutral strategies (e.g. statistical arbitrage)
would exploit these directions. In practice the model is neither complete nor
error-free, so orthogonal risk is understated and some regularization is needed,
without giving up the orthogonal space entirely.</p>
<p>skfolio provides three mechanisms to achieve this. The first is a
<a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.prior.CharacteristicsFactorModel.html#skfolio.prior.CharacteristicsFactorModel" title="skfolio.prior.CharacteristicsFactorModel"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">CharacteristicsFactorModel</span></code></a> parameter, the other two are
configured at the optimization step:</p>
<ul class="simple">
<li><p><code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">orthogonal_alpha_confidence</span></code> shrinks the orthogonal alpha point estimate toward
zero, reducing the incentive to allocate in orthogonal directions.</p></li>
<li><p><a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.uncertainty_set.OrthogonalMuUncertaintySet.html#skfolio.uncertainty_set.OrthogonalMuUncertaintySet" title="skfolio.uncertainty_set.OrthogonalMuUncertaintySet"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">OrthogonalMuUncertaintySet</span></code></a>, passed to the
optimizer’s <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">mu_uncertainty_set_estimator</span></code>, applies robust optimization to the
expected returns in the orthogonal space, penalizing allocations proportionally
to the uncertainty of the orthogonal alpha.</p></li>
<li><p><a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.uncertainty_set.OrthogonalCovarianceUncertaintySet.html#skfolio.uncertainty_set.OrthogonalCovarianceUncertaintySet" title="skfolio.uncertainty_set.OrthogonalCovarianceUncertaintySet"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">OrthogonalCovarianceUncertaintySet</span></code></a>, passed to
the optimizer’s <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">covariance_uncertainty_set_estimator</span></code>, inflates the covariance
in orthogonal directions, raising their variance directly.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>An optimizer can allocate in orthogonal directions even when the orthogonal alpha
is zero as binding constraints (e.g. factor-neutrality, long-only) act through
shadow prices and push weights into the orthogonal space. In a model without an
alpha estimator, such allocations carry idiosyncratic risk with no expected
reward, making the covariance-side regularization relevant beyond the alpha term
itself. The <a class="reference internal" href="#factor-model-portfolio-construction"><span class="std std-ref">Portfolio Construction</span></a>
section shows the optimizer-level configuration, and the regularization strength
(<code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">radius</span></code>) can be selected by walk-forward evaluation or
<a class="reference internal" href="#factor-model-hyper-parameter-tuning"><span class="std std-ref">hyperparameter tuning</span></a>.</p>
</section>
<section id="alpha-forecast-diagnostics">
<h3>Alpha Forecast Diagnostics<a class="headerlink" href="#alpha-forecast-diagnostics" title="Link to this heading">#</a></h3>
<p>Alpha research can be organized in two ways. The alpha estimator can either be developed
jointly with the factor model, attached through <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">alpha_estimator</span></code> and evaluated
end to end or it can be developed independently by first fitting a factor model without
an alpha estimator and then adding its fitted outputs to the panel with
<a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.prior.FactorModel.html#skfolio.prior.FactorModel.enrich_asset_panel" title="skfolio.prior.FactorModel.enrich_asset_panel"><code class="xref py py-meth docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">enrich_asset_panel</span></code></a> and finally iterating over
the alpha estimator directly using the enriched panel. The independent workflow avoids
refitting the factor model at each research iteration and is used below.</p>
<p>The <a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.alpha.alpha_forecast_evaluation.html#skfolio.alpha.alpha_forecast_evaluation" title="skfolio.alpha.alpha_forecast_evaluation"><code class="xref py py-func docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">alpha_forecast_evaluation</span></code></a> function evaluates the
historical forecasts produced by an alpha estimator against a forward target
field in an <a class="reference internal" href="../generated/skfolio.containers.AssetPanel.html#skfolio.containers.AssetPanel" title="skfolio.containers.AssetPanel"><code class="xref py py-class docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">AssetPanel</span></code></a>. The default target is
<code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">idio_returns</span></code>, the part of returns not explained by the factor model. The
function fits the estimator with <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">fit_transform</span></code>, then compares the alpha
forecasts at observation <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(t\)</span> with the forward mean target over
<span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\([t + \ell, t + \ell + h)\)</span>, where <span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(h\)</span> is <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">holding_period</span></code> and
<span class="math notranslate nohighlight">\(\ell\)</span> is <code class="docutils literal notranslate"><span class="pre">signal_lag</span></code>:</p>
<div class="highlight-python notranslate"><div class="highlight"><pre><span></span><span class="kn">from</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="nn">skfolio.utils.stats</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="kn">import</span> <span class="n">CSWeighting</span>
<span class="kn">from</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="nn">skfolio.alpha</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="kn">import</span> <span class="n">alpha_forecast_evaluation</span>

<span class="n">characteristics_enriched</span> <span class="o">=</span> <span class="n">factor_model</span><span class="o">.</span><span class="n">enrich_asset_panel</span><span class="p">(</span><span class="n">characteristics</span><span class="p">)</span>

<span class="n">evaluation</span> <span class="o">=</span> <span class="n">alpha_forecast_evaluation</span><span class="p">(</span>
    <span class="n">alpha_estimator</span><span class="p">,</span>
    <span class="n">characteristics_enriched</span><span class="p">,</span>
    <span class="n">holding_period</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="mi">5</span><span class="p">,</span>
    <span class="n">signal_lag</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="mi">1</span><span class="p">,</span>
    <span class="n">cs_weighting</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="n">CSWeighting</span><span class="o">.</span><span class="n">REGRESSION</span><span class="p">,</span>
    <span class="n">quantiles</span><span class="o">=</span><span class="p">(</span><span class="mf">0.1</span><span class="p">,</span> <span class="mf">0.25</span><span class="p">),</span>
<span class="p">)</span>

<span class="n">evaluation</span><span class="o">.</span><span class="n">ic_summary</span><span class="p">()</span>
</pre></div>
</div>
